Round 1

Q (Strategist): What is CUSTOM APP in CORE: a one-off YEP module or a reusable area for client apps? A: Reusable area, YEP first. A CUSTOM APP module that can hold client apps; YEP registration is the first, built as a reusable event-registration engine (event, guests, tables) with YEP 2026 as its first event.

Q (Architect/Guardian): Staff sign-in: CORE accounts with event roles, or separate event-only logins? A: CORE accounts + event roles. Personnel get CORE accounts (possibly a limited "Event staff" account type that only opens CUSTOM APP); the Super Administrator is one designated account per event. Reuse CORE sign-in, 2-step, sessions and audit.

Q (Skeptic): What does a guest type on the common QR page to identify themselves? A: Name + last 4 digits of mobile (or email). One exact match checks them in; anything else goes to staff-assisted search. Nothing listed publicly.

Q (Operator): Where does the event run on 9 December? A: The current CORE production server on EC2 (same domain, guest page e.g. /event/<code>); freeze deploys on event day; load-test beforehand.

Interview summary

Interview Summary: YEP 2026 Registration & Table Assignment as CORE "CUSTOM APP"

Core Intent

Build, inside CORE, a reusable "CUSTOM APP" module whose first app is the Legati Group YEP 2026 registration and table-assignment system (spec: spec.txt), ready and acceptance-tested before 9 December 2026 (12 days from today). This session is analysis only: how it will be built, with patterns from the n8n repository.

Key Decisions Made

  • CUSTOM APP is a reusable area for client-specific apps; YEP registration is the first app, built as a reusable event engine (events, guests, tables, delegation) with YEP 2026 as its first event.
  • Staff are CORE user accounts with event roles; possibly a limited "Event staff" account type that opens only CUSTOM APP. Exactly one Super Administrator per event, designated among CORE accounts; reuse CORE sign-in, 2-step, sessions, audit.
  • Guests self-verify on a common QR page with surname + last 4 digits of mobile (or email); single exact match only; else staff-assisted search. The master list is never exposed publicly.
  • Runs on the current CORE production (EC2, same domain); deploy freeze on event day; load test beforehand.

Open Questions for Deliberation

  • Concurrency: CORE uses a single-process JSON-file DB (db.data/db.save). How to guarantee "last seat: only one succeeds", atomic transfers/swaps, stale-write rejection, and permission changes taking effect immediately? (Node single thread may make a synchronous check-and-assign atomic; is that enough? versions/ETags per guest/table?)
  • Live sync to many devices: SSE vs polling; what n8n does (push via SSE/WebSocket); CORE has no push today.
  • Guest-facing public page: CSP, rate limits, enumeration resistance, status retrieval token after check-in, registration activation toggle.
  • Data model: events, guests (stable id), tables (classification, capacity 10, owner), seats, reservations, holds, delegations, approvals/substitutions, notifications, change history.
  • Recommendation engine: industry categories and relationships, group fit, eligibility by classification/table type, exclusions.
  • Email: CORE's existing mail/outbox (helpdesk SMTP) reuse; delivery status tracking and resend.
  • Offline contingency: printable seating plan and guest list, pre-partitioned ranges, reconciliation.
  • MVP vs optional within 12 days; acceptance-test scenarios automated (multi-device, concurrency).
  • "Reusable" without over-engineering: what is generic vs YEP-specific.
  • Patterns to borrow from n8n: Form Trigger (public forms), webhook auth, queue mode/concurrency control, push (SSE/WS), credentials and email nodes, execution locking.

Round 1: Position

ArchitectComplete
View position

Architect Position — CUSTOM APP module and the YEP 2026 Registration & Table Assignment engine

Core recommendation: Build CUSTOM APP as a thin CORE module shell (customapp) whose first app is a generic event engine. Store the event in CORE's existing db.json as four collections: events, tables, guests (the seat embedded in the guest), and an append-only change log. Route every state change through one synchronous unit-of-work, commit(). It re-reads the actor's event role, checks guest revisions, checks the capacity and scope invariants, applies the change, saves, and only then wakes waiting devices and queues email. Run live sync as a long-poll on a per-event sequence number inside the existing api.handle pipeline, not as a new SSE or WebSocket server. Keep the process single: Node's single thread is the concurrency guarantee, so anything that splits work across processes (n8n queue mode, a cluster) is ruled out.

Key argument: The spec's hardest requirements are concurrency requirements: the last seat, simultaneous check-in, atomic transfers and swaps, stale and unauthorized writes refused at the server, and permission changes taking effect at once. CORE handles them better than it first appears. api.handle (server/api.js:1432) resolves user from db.data.users on every request. A route handler that does no await between validation and db.save() (server/db.js:123, a synchronous write of a temp file followed by a rename) runs as one uninterruptible turn of the event loop. That is serializable isolation for free, as long as we never give it up.

The design therefore turns the invariants into structure wherever possible, and checks the rest in one place:

  • The seat lives inside the guest record (guest.seat = {tableId, seatNo, state}). One guest can hold at most one seat by construction, and a transfer is a single field change, so a valid seat is never released before its replacement is secured.
  • The owner is a single field on each guest and each table (ownerUserId), so double delegation cannot be represented.
  • The Super Administrator is a single field on the event (superAdminUserId), so "exactly one" holds by construction.
  • Capacity, seat-number uniqueness, eligibility and scope are checked by commit() against current in-memory state, immediately before the write.

Every command carries the guest revision it was based on (expect.guestRevs). Every refusal is a typed 409 (stale, full, seat_taken, out_of_scope) that includes fresh options, so the losing device refreshes as the spec requires. Queues are derived views of the data (owned, checked in, not yet seated), never stored lists. Reallocating staff is therefore just a change of owner: the new owner's queue fills immediately, and confirmed seats are untouched.

The same seq that orders the change log drives live sync. One mechanism gives four results:

  • devices refresh in under a second,
  • a restart costs nothing (the sequence number is saved with the data),
  • offline paper allocations can be replayed as commands through the same commit(), and
  • the event can be audited and reconstructed afterwards.

From n8n (sparse-cloned and read in this session), I borrow four patterns:

  • Push as a typed nudge (packages/cli/src/push/abstract.push.ts): one connection per user and tab replaces the older one, with a heartbeat.
  • Respond on receipt, then a waiting page (Form Trigger responseMode: onReceived): the guest's check-in answers immediately and a status page waits for the seat.
  • A token bound to one guest and one event (verifyFormUserAuthToken binds node, webhook and execution): our guest status token is bound to the guest and the event.
  • A capacity counter with a FIFO queue (concurrency/concurrency-queue.ts), used for outgoing email only.

I reject n8n's queue mode (Bull/Redis workers) because it would destroy the single-thread atomicity this design depends on.

Risks if ignored:

  • Structural, data level. If capacity is checked in the browser, or there is an await (email, file I/O) between the capacity check and db.save(), or capacity is stored as a counter beside the seats, the "last seat" and "no double allocation" guarantees are silently lost. Those are the two acceptance scenarios the client will test first. The same applies if tables and guests are modelled as two independently written lists (a seats[] on each table and a tableId on each guest): they can drift and double-count.
  • Integration level: an existing limit will block the event. In server/api.js:1445-1447, every non-GET /api/public/* call counts against public-write (20 per hour per IP), and every signed-out call counts against api-anon (120 per minute per IP). At the venue, most guests share one Wi-Fi IP, or a carrier-grade NAT on mobile data. Self check-in would start failing after about the 20th guest, and guests' waiting pages would be throttled. The event endpoints must be taken out of those generic buckets and given their own limits, keyed by event and identity, before the load test.
  • Performance and blast radius. db.save() does JSON.stringify(data, null, 2) of the whole of CORE (HR, CRM, projects, document metadata) on every change, on the shared production instance. Event data is small (about 300 guests, under about 2 MB with history). At about one save per second at peak, the cost depends on production's current db.json size, which nobody has measured. It must be measured now. If a save takes more than about 50 ms, coalesce the email-status writes; only above about 20 MB consider a separate event data file, which would mean extending the backup cron, the offsite manifest (server/offsite.js:265 allows only three file names) and RESTORE.md. Bulk import and export must also never block the loop during arrivals.

Dependencies on other domains:

  • Skeptic / Guardian: exact rate-limit numbers and the lockout after repeated failed attempts per surname. The identifier rule: what "last 4 of the email" means, and normalising compound Filipino surnames ("dela Cruz"). Status token lifetime. PII shown to staff outside their own scope (I propose counts and industries only). Purging guest data after the event.
  • Advocate: the guest page states (verify, pending, seated, see the desk); the staff queue and board layout; the group-split confirmation; and whether a failed verification leaves an "assist" record (I propose not: the guest goes to the desk).
  • Operator / Tuner: production db.json size and save() timing now; the deploy freeze; a pre-event backup and restore drill; whether the security monitor treats 25-second long-polls as slow or anomalous requests; a load script with 300 guests behind one IP and 20 to 30 staff long-polls.
  • Craftsman: in-process race tests (Promise.all of handle calls); a guard test that commit.js contains no await; a Playwright journey with two browser contexts racing for the last seat.
  • Strategist: confirm that holds, SSE, the floor plan, SMS and advanced exclusions stay out of the core build (each can be added later without changing the data model).
  • Integration owner (helpdesk): smtpOptions() and canEmail() are private to the helpdesk closure (server/helpdesk.js:245-249). Expose them as a shared outgoing-mail transport so CUSTOM APP can send without a second SMTP configuration.
  • Client (ICD): the industry list and related-industry map; the venue's table inventory (and therefore the table count); and whether the Super Administrator alone releases preferred no-show seats, or whether release can be delegated within scope (spec section 22).

FactWhereDesign consequence

handle awaits the handler (await withActor(...))server/api.js:1450Async handlers are allowed (long-poll), but mutating handlers must be synchronous inside
db.save() is synchronous: stringify the whole object, write .tmp, renameSyncserver/db.js:123-128Saving inside commit() keeps validate, apply and persist in one turn of the event loop
No optimistic-concurrency (rev) pattern anywhere; the 409s are only for upload offsetsserver/forms.js:159, server/documents.js:361rev per guest and table, and seq per event, are new and owned by this module

2. n8n patterns: borrow or reject (read from a sparse clone of n8n-io/n8n master, 27 Nov 2026)

n8n pattern (file)What it doesDecision for CORE
Push abstraction (packages/cli/src/push/abstract.push.ts)Connections keyed by userId:pushRef; a newer connection closes the older one; ping every 60 s; typed {type, data}; sendToUsersBorrow the semantics: waiters keyed by (user, tab); a new wait resolves the old one; typed messages. Push carries only {seq} (an invalidation), and data comes from an authorized GET, so scope filtering exists in one place and no PII travels over push.
SSE backend (push/sse.push.ts)text/event-stream, :ok prelude, X-Accel-Buffering: no, removes the client on closeDefer. It needs a server.js hook outside api.handle, bypassing the monitor, limits and audit. Long-poll gives sub-second updates within the existing pipeline. If it is added after the event, copy this exact handshake.
Push origin validation (push/origin-validator.ts)An SSE GET without an Origin header passes only for a same-origin fetchCORE already enforces the host allow-list and cross-site checks (server.js:223-237); nothing to add for long-poll.
Form Trigger response modes (nodes/Form/common.descriptions.ts:605, onReceived, lastNode, responseNode)Answer at once, or hold until the workflow endsBorrow onReceived: verification answers immediately (checked in, pending) with a status token; a separate waiting page long-polls. Never hold the check-in POST open until a member of staff seats the guest.
Form auth token (nodes/Form/utils/utils.ts:790-860)HS256 JWT bound to node, webhook and executionBorrow the binding, not the JWT: a random 32-byte status token stored hashed on the guest is bound to (event, guest), can be revoked by re-issuing, and needs no signing key.
Sandboxed form CSP (utils.ts:679, getHtmlSandboxCSP)Strict CSP on public form pagesAlready matched by CORE's hash-based pagePolicy; event.html gets the same treatment.
ConcurrencyQueue (concurrency/concurrency-queue.ts)Capacity counter plus FIFO of waiting promises; throttled and released eventsBorrow for the email outbox (at most one or two SMTP sends at a time, FIFO). Reject for seats: the spec wants the loser to fail fast and refresh, not wait in a queue.
Capacity reservation (concurrency-capacity-reservation.ts)reserve() refuses a second reserve; release() is explicitThis is the model for optional seat holds if they are ever added: explicit release, never two holds per guest. Holds are out of the core scope, since commit() makes them unnecessary for correctness.
Queue mode (Bull/Redis workers), multi-main pub/sub (push/index.ts:231)Scale-out across processesReject. More than one process breaks the single-thread atomicity guarantee. CORE must stay one process; write that down as an operational rule.
Credentials encryptionEncrypted credential storeCORE already has server/secrets.js; no new secrets (SMTP is reused).

Note: the council repository named in the user's request is this agentic-council plugin. Its process is followed here; it adds no code patterns.

3. Boundaries (C4, container and component level)

CORE process (server.js, single Node, systemd)
 ├─ api.handle ─ authorize(route perm) ─ withActor ─ handler
 │    ├─ /api/customapp/...          (signed in; perm customapp.use; event role checked inside)
 │    └─ /api/public/event/:code/... (signed out; event-specific rate limits; registration toggle)
 └─ CUSTOM APP module  (server/customapp/)
      index.js     registerCustomAppRoutes(route, db, {audit, notify, mail})  ← app registry
      events/      THE EVENT ENGINE (app #1, id 'events')
        model.js     collection accessors, normalisers, lookup keys
        commit.js    unit of work: re-read role → idempotency → plan → expect → invariants → apply → save → after-commit
        commands.js  one plan() per command type (pure; reads state, returns writes or throws Conflict)
        feed.js      per-event seq, long-poll waiters Map<eventId, Map<user:tab, resolve>>
        outbox.js    email queue, ConcurrencyQueue-style drain, delivery status
        public.js    guest verify, status long-poll (no master-list reads leave this file)
        io.js        CSV/XLSX import with dedupe, CSV export, printable plan (server/pdf)
 shared/customapp.js        APPS registry, enums, validators (used by server and browser)
 shared/seating.js          eligibility, capacity, ranking: PURE functions, browser and server
 public/js/views/customapp*.js  staff and Super Administrator screens (SPA, #/customapp/...)
 public/event.html + public/js/event.js   standalone guest page (like support.html)

Ownership rules:

  • Only commit.js writes event state.
  • shared/seating.js never writes.
  • public.js can reach commit() only through a narrow selfCheckIn command.
  • Nothing outside server/customapp/ reads the ca* collections, apart from the admin export and backup, which take the whole file.

What is generic and what is YEP-specific. The CUSTOM APP shell is generic: a code-defined APPS list (no low-code or metadata platform). The event engine is generic for corporate seated events: classifications, industries, tables, ownership, check-in, email. YEP 2026 is pure data: one event record with its industries, tables, guests, staff and email wording. There are no if (event.code === 'yep...') branches, ever.

Not generalized now: multiple sessions per event, floor plan, tickets or payments, SMS, custom guest fields. These are extension points (event.settings, guest.tags), not abstractions.

Appendix A — Context briefing (codebase-context, scoped to CUSTOM APP)

Tech Stack
  • Framework: none. Raw node:http server (server.js) plus a hand-written router (server/api.js route()); vanilla ES-module SPA, no build step.
  • Language: JavaScript (ESM), Node 22 in production (package engines says 18 or later). CORE version 0.70.0-alpha.
  • Database: a single JSON document data/db.json, held in memory (db.data) and written atomically by db.save(); hash-chained audit-log.jsonl.
  • Auth: CORE sessions (cookie), scrypt passwords, TOTP 2-step, role permissions (shared/roles.js userCan), per-company role overrides.
  • Hosting: AWS EC2, Caddy (TLS, gzip) → 127.0.0.1:3000, systemd; hourly S3 offsite copy, nightly local backups, daily restore check.
  • Key Dependencies: none at runtime; Playwright for dev and CI only.
Architectural Patterns
  • Routing: server: route(method, '/api/x/:id', permission, handler); client: hash routes in public/js/app.js as [path, view, permission, moduleId].
  • Data fetching: the browser fetches JSON; it polls for badges; there is no push.
  • State management: the server holds authoritative in-memory state; the client re-fetches after every change.
  • Component pattern: one view file per module screen, built with helpers from ui.js.
Data Model (the existing parts this module touches)
EntityKey fieldsRelationships
usersid, username, role, active, 2FAevent.superAdminUserId, event.staff[].userId, guest/table.ownerUserId → users.id
roleOverridesrole → permissionsadds customapp.use / customapp.manage per company
modulesid → enabledcustomapp on/off; MODULE_API refuses /api/customapp and /api/public/event when off
helpdesk.outgoingSMTP settings (sealed password)reused by the event outbox through an extracted transport
audit-log.jsonlaction, entity, entityId, actorcustomapp.* actions
Conventions
  1. Flat collections in db.data, with a module prefix (crmDeals, pmTasks) and defaults through data.x ||= [] in openDb. This module uses caEvents, caTables, caGuests, caChanges, caOutbox.
  2. Handlers throw HttpError(status, message); success returns plain data ({data, headers} when headers are needed).
  3. Every visible change updates the guides plus whats-new, bumps APP_VERSION and adds a devlog entry; tests run with node --test against openDb(tmp) + createApi(db) + handle(...).
  4. IDs come from randomUUID(); human codes use counters in module config (here: YEP-0001 guest references per event).
Infrastructure Constraints
  • One process, one in-memory dataset shared with all of CORE. A crash or a slow save affects every module.
  • Every push to the branch deploys to production after CI, so the event-day freeze has to be enforced by process (branch protection), not just agreed.
  • There is no streaming route hook.
Integration Points
ServicePurposeAuth
SMTP (helpdesk outgoing)assignment emailsealed credentials (server/secrets.js), STARTTLS/TLS
S3 offsitehourly copy of db.json (includes event data)EC2 instance role
CaddyTLS, gzip, real client IPn/a
Warnings / Tech Debt
  • The generic public rate limits are per IP and will throttle a venue NAT (see Risks).
  • Whole-file save cost has not been measured on production.
  • There is no generic outbox or delivery-status store, so this module builds one inside its own area. It could be promoted to CORE later.

Derived, never stored:

  • assignmentStatus = none / reserved / confirmed / released
  • queue(owner) = guests owned by that person, checked in, not seated (or released)
  • superQueue = ownerUserId == null + unlisted pending + flagged + released-and-arrived
  • table counts = live seats at the table
caChanges (append-only)

{seq, eventId, at, actorId, via:'staff'|'self'|'reconcile', type, guestIds[], tableIds[], before, after, reason, requestId}

This one log serves as:

  • the spec's approval and change history (section 7),
  • the live-sync cursor,
  • the idempotency store (look up by requestId),
  • the reconciliation record, and
  • the post-event report.

CORE audit.record('customapp.<type>', ...) is written for the spec section 13 list: approvals, overrides, responsibility changes, releases, transfers and corrections.

caOutbox

{id, eventId, guestId, kind:'assignment'|'update'|'resend', to, subject, body (rendered at queue time from a seat snapshot), state:'queued'|'sending'|'sent'|'failed', attempts, lastError, createdAt, sentAt, createdBy}

A failure never touches guest.seat or guest.arrival (spec section 6).

Invariants (enforced in commit(); most are structural)
#InvariantHow
I1live seats(table) ≤ table.capacity ≤ event.hardLimit (10)checked against the post-state of every command
I2a guest holds at most one seatstructural (guest.seat is a single field)
I3(tableId, seatNo) is unique among live seatschecked
I4each guest and table has at most one owner; the owner is active staffstructural plus checked
I5exactly one Super Administrator per eventstructural
I6a substitute and the original never both hold a seatthe seat is moved inside one commit; checked
I7check-in happens oncestate-machine guard; idempotent replay
I8a table with live seats cannot be deactivated or shrunk below its countchecked
I9a reserved (preferred) seat never expiresno timer touches seats; only release clears one
I10the actor may write only guests and tables they own (the Super Administrator may write any)re-read from current state on every commit
Index strategy (in-memory)

The data is about 300 guests and about 40 tables, so a linear scan per check costs microseconds; no indexes are needed for correctness.

Add two lazily built maps per event, invalidated on seq change: liveSeatsByTable and guestsByLookupSurname. They are an optimisation for the board and verify paths only. Commit-time capacity checks recount from the source, so a stale cache can never allow an overbooking.

Access policy (the role-level security equivalent)
ResourceSuper AdministratorStaff (owner)Staff (not owner) / viewerGuest (public)
Event settings, industries, staff, delegationread/writereadreadname and open state only
Guest recordread/write allread/write own guestsnone (search for assisted check-in shows name, company and ref only)own first name, table and seat, via status token
Table seat-level detailallown tablescounts, industries and remaining seats only (read-only)none
Commandsallwithin scope (I10)noneselfCheckIn only
Migration (JSON)
  • Up: add customApp: {counters:{}}, caEvents: [], caTables: [], caGuests: [], caChanges: [] and caOutbox: [] to emptyData(), plus data.x ||= ... in openDb. Register the module entry, MODULE_API, the eventstaff role and the permissions customapp.use / customapp.manage.
  • Down: switch the module off (routes are refused at once); delete data.ca* and data.customApp; remove the role (users holding it fall back per existing role-removal handling). The data stays in the dated nightly backups.

Appendix C — The unit of work (server/customapp/events/commit.js)

// Synchronous by design: no await anywhere in this file (a test enforces it).
export function commit(ctx, eventId, user, cmd) {
  const ev = mustLiveEvent(ctx.db, eventId);
  const role = roleOf(ev, user);                     // re-read now: permission changes take effect immediately
  const prior = findChange(ev, cmd.requestId);       // network retry or double tap
  if (prior) return resultOf(prior);
  const plan = COMMANDS[cmd.type].plan(ctx.db, ev, role, cmd);  // pure; throws Conflict(code, fresh)
  expectRevs(plan, cmd.expect);                      // stale -> 409 {code:'stale', fresh}
  checkInvariants(ctx.db, ev, plan);                 // I1-I10 on the post-state -> 409 {code:'full'|'seat_taken'|'out_of_scope'|...}
  const undo = apply(ctx.db, ev, plan);              // mutate, rev++, ev.seq++, push caChanges
  try { ctx.db.save(); } catch (e) { undo(); throw new HttpError(503, 'Not saved. Try again.'); }
  afterCommit(ctx, ev, plan);                        // audit.record, feed.wake(ev.id), outbox.enqueue (no awaits)
  return plan.result;                                // includes new revs and seq
}

The command set is one endpoint, so the code has one place for revalidation (heuristic: minimise API surface):

typeWhoNotes
checkIn, undoCheckInowner, Super Administratorfor preferred guests, checkIn turns a reserved seat into confirmed in the same commit; a released preferred guest goes to the Super Administrator's queue
assign {placements:[{guestId, tableId, seatNo?}], allowSplit}owner within scope, Super Administratora group is one commit for all members; splitting across tables requires allowSplit:true (staff confirmation)
transfer, swapscope covers all guests and tables involved; otherwise Super Administratorone commit; capacity checked on the destination only
reserve (preferred, before the event), releaseSuper Administrator (owner if releaseDelegable)never automatic
createUnlisted, approve, rejectstaff create, Super Administrator decidesapproval records identity, classification, industry and owner
substitute {originalId, newGuest, moveSeat}Super Administrator or authorised staffthe seat moves; the original is marked substitutedBy
delegateGuests, delegateTables {ids, toUserId\null}Super Administratorfor guests, only pending (unseated) ones move unless forced; seats are never touched
table.add, table.update, table.relocateSuper AdministratorI8 enforced; relocate and deactivate can be one commit
notify {guestIds, kind}owner, Super Administratorqueues outbox messages; no state change other than notify

The public selfCheckIn uses the same commit() with a synthetic actor (via:'self'), and its plan is restricted to checkIn.

Appendix D — Route contract (minimum surface)

Signed in, permission customapp.use (customapp.manage for creating events and setting the Super Administrator):

Method & pathReturns / does
GET /api/customapp/appsregistry for the module landing page
GET/POST /api/customapp/eventslist the events the caller has a role in / create (manage)
GET/PATCH /api/customapp/events/:idsettings, industries, staff (Super Administrator); PATCH carries rev
POST /api/customapp/events/:id/super-admincustomapp.manage plus re-authentication (sensitive bucket); audited; notifies the old and new holder
GET /api/customapp/events/:id/board?since=<seq>&wait=25&tab=<id>live sync. Answers at once with {seq, board} if seq > since, else holds up to 25 s and returns {seq, unchanged:true}. board is scoped per Appendix B: my queue, my tables, read-only availability, Super Administrator queues and the dashboard
GET /api/customapp/events/:id/recommend?guestIds=a,b,cranked eligible tables (Appendix F), split into mine and othersReadOnly
GET /api/customapp/events/:id/guests?q=&class=&status=&table=search and filter (scoped) for assisted check-in
POST /api/customapp/events/:id/commands{type, requestId, expect:{guestRevs}, ...} → commit()
POST /api/customapp/events/:id/import (CSV/XLSX) → preview; POST .../import/:jobId/applytwo-step import with a dedupe report; apply is one commit
GET `/api/customapp/events/:id/export.csv?kind=guests\seating\registrations and /plan.pdf`export and printable offline plan

Signed out (/api/public/event/:code/..., gated by event live + registrationOpen, event-specific limits, never the generic per-IP buckets):

Method & pathReturns
GET /api/public/event/:code{name, date, open}, nothing else
POST /api/public/event/:code/verify {surname, last4}exactly one match → selfCheckIn (idempotent) → {token}. Anything else → a uniform {ok:false, see:'desk'} with the same response time and shape
GET /api/public/event/:code/status/:token?since=&wait=25`{state:'pending'\'seated'\'see_desk', firstName, table, seat}`

Pages: /event/<code> and /event/<code>/s/<token> → public/event.html (added to server.js page routing and to pagePolicy).

Error contract: 409 {error, code, fresh} where code is one of stale, full, seat_taken, out_of_scope, not_eligible, already_checked_in, needs_super_admin. fresh carries refreshed options so the client re-renders without a second call.

Appendix E — Live sync

  • ev.seq increments in apply(); feed.wake(ev.id) resolves every waiter for that event after the save.
  • Waiters are keyed userId:tab (the n8n connectionKey idea); a new wait from the same tab resolves the previous one as unchanged. Each user has a cap on open waits.
  • Load: 30 staff devices plus about 60 guests waiting at once, at one request per 25 s each when idle, is about 4 requests per second. Under change, each commit wakes everyone once, and a scoped board is about 10-100 KB before gzip. Node handles this easily on one core.
  • Restart or deploy: the waits drop and clients re-poll with since, losing no state. A client whose since is ahead of the server's seq (a restored backup) receives a full board.
  • Fallback: if a wait errors, the client falls back to a 3-second plain poll of the same URL with wait=0. One contract, two speeds.
  • Operator must check that the security monitor (server/monitor.js) does not flag 25-second holds as anomalies, and must confirm the intended session effect: the board's long-poll keeps staff signed in during the event.

Appendix F — Recommendation engine (shared/seating.js, pure)

recommend({event, tables, guests, group:[guestIds], scope}) → [{tableId, tier, free, reasons[], selectable}]
eligible(table, guest): active && classOK(table.classification, guest.classification, settings.mixedTables)
                        && free(table) ≥ group.length && !excluded(table, group)
classOK: preferred→{preferred, mixed}; regular→{regular, mixed}; employee→{employee, mixed}; unlisted→per approval classification
tier 1: same industry already seated at the table · tier 2: related industry · tier 3: other eligible
order within a tier: fits the whole group first → more same-industry companions → fewer seats left after placement (fills gaps, does not force sequential filling)
selectable = scope covers the table (I10); others are returned read-only for visibility (spec section 13)
reasons: human strings ('2 from Banking', 'related: Insurance', '4 seats left') for the staff screen

The same eligible() is called by commit() as a constraint. Ranking stays advisory: a staff member may pick any eligible table in scope. If no group-fit table exists, the result includes split suggestions (the largest fitting partitions), and the client must send allowSplit:true. If nothing is eligible, the result is {none:true, refer:'super_admin'}.

Appendix G — Email

  • afterCommit queues an assignment or update message when a seat is confirmed or changed and an email address exists. The body is rendered from a seat snapshot when it is queued.
  • outbox.js drains through a capacity-1 FIFO (the n8n ConcurrencyQueue shape): it marks sending, saves, await sendMail(transport(), ...), then marks sent or failed plus guest.notify, and saves. The await happens outside any commit(), and the status writes touch only caOutbox and guest.notify.
  • Retries: three with backoff on transient SMTP errors, then failed; resend is a notify command. A restart re-queues any message left in sending older than 2 minutes, accepting a rare duplicate email over a lost one.
  • Transport: extract smtpOptions() and canEmail() from helpdesk.js into a shared mailTransport(db); the event sets its own From name and reply-to.

Appendix H — Offline contingency and reconciliation

  • Exclusive table ownership already is the "pre-partitioned offline seating range": each member of staff may seat only at their own tables, on paper, and the Super Administrator acts as the single authority for anything else.
  • Before the event, and at the start of event day, export the following and print them: the guest list (ref, name, company, class, owner, reserved table and seat), the seating plan per table, and a blank allocation sheet per owner.
  • After reconnecting, staff enter paper allocations through the commands endpoint with via:'reconcile' and occurredAt. They pass through the same commit(), so a paper overbooking is refused and listed, never applied. The Super Administrator resolves the refusals, then flips registrationOpen back on.
  • There is no client-side offline database or service worker in 12 days; the risk is greater than the benefit.

Appendix I — Build order (architecture-critical path, 12 days)

  1. Data model and migration, shared/seating.js, commit() with invariants, and pure and race tests (this is the foundation everything else assumes).
  2. Commands endpoint plus board long-poll; the Super Administrator setup screens (tables, industries, staff, delegation, import).
  3. Public verify and status page plus the event-specific limits (with a NAT load test).
  4. Outbox and email; export and printable plan.
  5. Dashboard and polish, then guides, journeys and the load test. Freeze about two days before the event.

Out of the core scope, with no data-model change needed to add later: seat holds, SSE, floor plan, SMS, advanced exclusion rules, analytics.

AdvocateComplete
View position

Advocate Position: YEP 2026 Registration & Table Assignment as CORE "CUSTOM APP"

Core recommendation: Design around two moments that have to feel instant under pressure. The first is the guest at reception, in formal wear on a phone with weak signal, going from QR to "Table 7, Seat 4" in under 30 seconds. The second is the registration staffer with a line in front of them seating one guest in under 15 seconds and two taps. Build three separate surfaces: a tiny standalone guest page (not the SPA), a phone-first "My queue" screen that is the landing page for Event staff, and a desktop/tablet "Event control" screen for the Super Administrator. Every server rejection (last seat taken, guest moved to someone else, registration closed) must come back as a recovery screen with fresh options, never as a bare error toast.

Key argument: Walk through 9 December. It is 6:40 PM. Forty guests come off the elevators in ten minutes, most of them on the venue Wi-Fi. Mrs. Dela Cruz scans the QR on the easel, types "dela cruz" and "4417", and the page has to cope with "Dela Cruz", "De la Cruz", "DELA CRUZ" and "Dela Cruz-Reyes". She is preferred, so she gets the delight moment straight away: "Welcome, Ms. Dela Cruz. Table 3, Seat 4". The number is huge, an email follows, and nobody else had to act. Mr. Tan is regular. He sees "You're checked in. We're choosing your table. This page updates by itself." He hasn't been rejected, he is waiting, and his status token page (like /support/t/<token>) refreshes without him touching it. Meanwhile Joel, a staffer holding a phone, gets a new card at the top of his queue: "Tan, Robert · Megabank · Banking · waiting 0:20". He taps it and sees three ranked tables, each with a reason ("Same industry: Banking, 3 of 6 seated") and a 10-dot capacity meter. He taps Table 5, then Confirm, and he is done. If Ana took the last seat at Table 5 a second earlier, Joel must not see "Error 409". He must see "Table 5 just filled. Updated options:" with the list re-ranked in place and Mr. Tan still selected. The spec states the requirements: recommendations are advisory, write access is scoped, there is one Super Administrator, last seat goes to one person. Whether people experience those requirements as safety or as friction comes down to these recovery states. The guest's other paths need the same care. When there is no match or an ambiguous match, the page must not dead-end with "not found". It should show "Please see the registration desk and show this code: R-482". That referral code carries what the guest typed, so the staffer's assisted search starts pre-filled. The same referral path covers walk-ins, substitutes and released reservations. One more point resolves a real tension in the spec: any event staffer may check a guest in at the desk; only the owner (or the Super Administrator) may seat them. Assisted search at reception has to find anyone, but delegation governs seating, not arrival. A checked-in guest who belongs to someone else drops into their owner's queue, and the staffer at the desk says so: "You're checked in; Maria will have your table in a moment."

Risks if ignored:

  • Launch blocker found in the code: the existing public rate limits will refuse real guests. server/api.js:1447 caps every non-GET to /api/public/* at 20 per hour per IP (public-write), and api-anon allows 120 GETs/min per IP. At the venue, guests on hotel Wi-Fi share one NAT address. The 21st guest's check-in gets "Too many messages from this network". Fifty guests whose status pages poll every 5 s add up to 600 requests/min and lock out everyone. The event routes need their own buckets: a high per-IP ceiling sized for one shared address, plus per-event and per-surname failure caps to stop guessing. The status page must poll lightly with backoff, for example 4 s for the first 2 minutes and then 10 s, paused when the tab is hidden. This needs a load test from one IP.
  • Identity matching that fails real Filipino names creates a queue at the desk. If matching is strictly literal, compound surnames (dela/de la/de los), ñ and accents, hyphenated married names, "Ma." and "Jr." all fall to assisted search, and assisted search is the slowest path. Normalize case, diacritics, spaces, hyphens, periods and apostrophes, and match on the stored surname plus any alias. Married-versus-listed-name mismatches still go to the desk by design. Also, "last 4 of email" is not a real identifier, because most addresses end in ".com". Offer "last 4 digits of mobile" as the main field and "or your full email address" as the alternative.
  • Silent state changes will make staff distrust the system mid-event. A guest who disappears from Joel's queue because the Super Administrator reassigned them, a write refused because his delegation was revoked, a table that greys out under his thumb: each needs an explanation in place. Examples: "Moved to Maria's queue by the Super Administrator, 6:52 PM"; "You no longer manage Table 9". Without that, staff phone the command desk at the busiest moment of the night. Missing keyboard/screen-reader support and low contrast in a dim ballroom are smaller risks, but they are real for older executives using the guest page.

Dependencies on other domains:

  • Architect: split check-in from seat assignment as separate state transitions and endpoints. A conflict response (409) must carry the fresh recommendation list and who or what won, so the client can re-render without a second round trip. Per-record rev with refusal reasons as stable codes (seat_taken, not_owner, guest_moved, stale) that the UI maps to copy. A referral-attempt entity (short code, inputs typed, timestamp). A guest status token. A since-revision delta feed for queues.
  • Skeptic: confirm that re-verifying from a second device shows the same status (idempotent, low-sensitivity: table number only) rather than an "already checked in" error, and that the uniform no-match/multi-match response does not leak existence. Confirm that full email is acceptable as the alternative identifier.
  • Tuner/Operator: the venue-NAT rate-limit problem above; poll intervals for about 10 staff devices plus up to about 300 guest status pages; venue Wi-Fi survey and a 4G hotspot for staff; printed event-day pack ritual; deploy freeze.
  • Craftsman: Playwright journeys at 390 px phone viewport with 2+ browser contexts racing for the last seat, plus a guest-page journey through closed, no match, preferred, and pending then seated.
  • Strategist: agree the cut line below (graphical floor plan, seat-picker visuals, undo and sounds are follow-ups).
MVP vs follow-up (UX lens, honest about the 12 days)
Must ship (launch blockers)Follow-up / nice to have
Guest page: closed state, verify, preferred result, pending to seated auto-refresh, referral code, no-match pathAnimated reveal, wake-lock, add-to-calendar
Event-specific rate-limit buckets that survive one shared venue IPSMS
Staff "My queue" (phone-first) with wait timers, 2-tap seat, conflict recovery, moved/revoked explanationsSound/vibration on new arrival; undo within 10 s

Seat number as a simple 1–10 chip row (mandatory for preferred)Seat visualization around a round table
Email status chip (Sent / Failed / No email) + Resend, never blockingDelivery analytics
Print event-day pack (A–Z list, per-table sheets, per-staffer sheets, "as of" timestamp) + reconciliation entryPrinted slips per guest
Operator guide + CORE docs/guides page for Event staff & SA (test/guides.test.js requires coverage of sidebar items)Video walkthrough

Step 4: Alternate paths
  • First-time vs returning guest: a returning visit (re-scan, email link) always lands on "my status". Re-verifying shows the same status with no "already checked in" error. The duplicate is still prevented, and staff see "verified again from another device" on the record.
  • Group arrival: the staffer turns on select mode (a checkbox on each card, which works on touch and keyboard, unlike long-press), picks 3, then "Seat together (3)". Only tables with ≥3 free spaces are ranked. If none fit: "No table has 3 seats. Options: Table 5 (2) + Table 8 (1)". Splitting needs an explicit "Split group" confirm. A group spanning owners shows "Ask the Super Administrator", and a one-tap "Send group to SA" moves it.
  • Power user (SA): desktop table grid plus a filterable guest list (name, org, industry, class, status, table) with CSV/Excel export. Bulk delegation before the event works by selecting guests by filter, then "Assign to Joel", and selecting tables by range ("Tables 10–15 to Joel").
  • Mobile vs desktop: staff queue is designed for 390 px first (bottom sheet, thumb-reach Confirm). The SA screen is designed for ≥1024 px first and collapses to tabbed sections on a tablet (Exceptions / Tables / People). This is a separate design, not a squeezed one (heuristic 4).
  • Substitute: from the original invitee's record, "Record substitute" opens a pre-filled form linked to the original. The UI shows one seat moving ("Seat 3-4: Juan Reyes replaces Pedro Reyes"), never two people on one seat.

Step 6: Friction points and mitigations
  • Friction: surname typing on a phone with formatting variants. Mitigation: normalization plus alias surnames; examples in placeholder text ("e.g. Dela Cruz").
  • Friction: "last 4 of email" is meaningless. Mitigation: mobile last-4 is the primary field; full email is the alternative.
  • Friction: guest in "pending" with no sense of progress. Mitigation: an explicit sentence, an "updated" timestamp, and a 4-minute escalation prompt that matches the staff amber timer.
  • Friction: staff unsure whether they may seat a guest. Mitigation: out-of-scope tables render read-only with a "Not yours" label, and the server enforces the same rule.
  • Friction: assisted search shows personal data. Mitigation: results show name, company and status only. Mobile/email appear only as masked last 4 for confirmation ("•••• 4417").
  • Friction: shared venue IP throttling (see Risks). Mitigation: event-specific buckets plus a load test from a single IP.
  • Friction: dim ballroom and small text. Mitigation: ≥16 px inputs (also stops iOS zoom), 48 px targets, WCAG AA contrast in both CORE themes, table number at ≥56 px.
Step 7: Delight moments
  • Delight: preferred guests see their table and seat within one tap of the QR. It is the express lane made visible.
  • Delight: the pending screen turns into "Table 5" by itself, with a short highlight, while the guest is still at the desk.
  • Delight: the reason line on a recommendation ("Same industry: Banking, 3 of 6 seated") lets staff say something useful to the guest ("You'll be with other bankers").
  • Delight: the email subject reads "Your table at YEP 2026: Table 5", so it is readable from the lock screen.

Appendix B: Screen inventory and key interaction specs (for Architect/Craftsman)

  1. Guest page /event/<code> (standalone HTML on CORE styles, CSP-hash-compatible, noindex)
  2. States: closed · verify · submitting · seated (preferred) · pending · referred (code) · released · offline.
  3. A11y: labelled inputs, autocomplete="off", inputmode="numeric" + pattern="\d{4}", errors linked via aria-describedby, result region aria-live="polite", focus moves to result heading, works fully by keyboard, honors prefers-reduced-motion for the highlight.
  4. Status page /event/<code>/s/<token>: the same component in read-only form. Polls with backoff and pauses on visibilitychange.
  5. Staff: My queue (Event staff landing page)
  6. Tabs/sections: Waiting for table (n) · Checked in, seated (n) · Find guest. A sticky top bar shows the event name, the staffer's name and "Last updated 2 s ago". If updates stop for more than 15 s, the bar turns amber and reads "Reconnecting".
  7. Card: name, company, industry chip, class badge (Preferred/Regular/Employee/Unlisted, with text and not color alone), wait timer that turns amber at 4 min and red at 8 min, group link icon.
  8. Guest sheet: recommendations (max 3 shown) → other eligible → read-only others; seat chips 1–10; Confirm button repeats the choice.
  9. Staff: Find guest / assisted check-in: search by partial name, company or referral code. Results are minimal. Actions: "Check in" (any staffer) and "Seat" (only if owner). Also "Add walk-in" and "Record substitute".
  10. SA: Event control: counts row (Invited · RSVP yes · Checked in · Not yet arrived · Waiting for table · Needs approval · Seats left). Table tiles grid shows a number, class color plus label, 10-dot meter (filled = seated, ring = reserved/held), industries represented, and owner. Full tiles are dimmed with a "Full" label. Below that: Exceptions inbox, per-staffer workload (pending count, longest wait, last active), and "Redistribute pending…".
  11. SA: Setup (pre-event): Import (Excel/CSV with column mapping preview and duplicate report), Guests, Tables (bulk create "Tables 1–30, capacity 10"), Industries & relationships, Delegation (bulk by filter/range with overlap prevented at selection time), Registration on/off with opening-time message, Print pack, Export.
  12. Print pack (@media print, which CORE already uses in 7 places in styles.css): A–Z list with table/seat, per-table sheets, per-staffer sheets, and a pre-partitioned free-seat range per staffer for outage mode. Each page is stamped "as of <time>" and "Print again after changes".
Staff conflict-recovery contract (what the UI needs from the API)
409 { code: "seat_taken" | "table_full" | "not_owner" | "guest_moved" | "stale" | "no_eligible_seat",
      message: "<human sentence>",
      by?: { name, at },              // who/what won, if shareable
      guest: <current guest state>,   // so the sheet re-renders in place
      options?: <fresh ranked tables> // for seat_taken / table_full
    }
Copy principles
  • Address the guest by title and surname. Write sentences, not codes. Never show "error", "invalid" or "not found" to a guest.
  • Staff copy names the person and the time ("by the Super Administrator, 6:52 PM").
  • Every guest-facing dead end points to a human: "Please see the registration desk."
Guides (CORE convention)

Add a CORE guide (e.g. docs/guides/custom-app-events.md, roles: event staff type + admin, apps: customapp) with sections "Seat a guest", "When a table fills up", "Find a guest at the desk", "Event control (Super Administrator)", "If the system is down". Add a whats-new.md line and screenshots in screenshots.json. This doubles as the spec's required "brief operator guide". A one-page printable desk card for staff is recommended.

SkepticComplete
View position

Skeptic Position: CUSTOM APP / YEP 2026 Registration and Table Assignment

Lens: red. Pre-mortem, attack surface, Swiss cheese, Chesterton's fence. Grounding: spec.txt, interview summary, codebase map, plus direct reads of server/api.js (rate limiter buckets ~554, request path ~1433), server/auth.js (createSessions), shared/roles.js (userCan), server/security.js (session policy), server/db.js (save), server/helpdesk.js (public form, IMAP mailbox), .github/workflows/deploy-ec2.yml, deploy/aws/update.sh. n8n caveat: the n8n repository is not present in this workspace and could not be read. Every n8n reference below is from model knowledge and is marked (unverified).

Core recommendation

The concurrency story is the easy part. Node's single thread plus a synchronous validate-then-write handler gives the "last seat, only one wins" guarantee almost for free. The real event-day failures hide in CORE's existing safeguards, which were built for an office ERP, not a ballroom with 300 phones on one Wi-Fi address:

  • the per-IP rate limits would lock the guest page after about 20 guests;
  • CORE admins' * permission breaks "exactly one Super Administrator";
  • sessions are held in memory, so any restart signs every staff device out at peak.

Fix those three before building any feature. Then make one pure "plan, validate invariants, apply, save" function the only path that can change a seat.

Key argument

Pre-mortem: it is 9 December, 18:40, and arrivals are peaking. Here is how it fails.

  1. Venue NAT. Guests on the venue Wi-Fi share one public IP. server/api.js:1447 charges every

non-GET /api/public/* call to the global public-write bucket (20 per hour per IP), and every signed-out GET to api-anon (120 per minute per IP). Guest 21 gets "Too many messages from this network". If 300 guests each poll their status every 5 seconds, they generate about 3,600 requests a minute, so the pending-status screen dies within seconds.

  1. Staff logins share the same address. auth (20 per minute per IP) and signin-fail

(20 per 15 minutes per address) apply to staff on that Wi-Fi too. Fifteen staff re-signing in with password and 2-step after a restart is about 30 auth hits, which trips the limit.

  1. Restarts lose every session. createSessions keeps sessions in a Map in memory, and

update.sh does systemctl stop/start. Any deploy, crash or out-of-memory restart signs every staff device out at once. Then the NAT limits in step 2 lock them out.

  1. Pushes reach production. Every push to claude/busy-curie-hfpbg0 runs the deploy pipeline,

and other sessions push to this branch. The 0.38 approval gate (environment: production) is the only thing between a routine commit and an event-day restart.

  1. Too many unrestricted accounts. userCan returns true for role: 'admin' on any permission.

If event authority is modeled as a CORE permission, every CORE admin is a second unrestricted Super Administrator. The spec forbids that in sections 1, 11 and 13.

  1. Shared database, shared save. db.save() pretty-prints the whole shared db.json

synchronously on every mutation. At arrival bursts, the event loop stalls on saves. Staff double-tap. Without idempotency keys, a double-tap after a slow response becomes a second mutation: a transfer applied twice, or a group confirmed twice.

None of these is a seating-logic bug. All of them would pass a unit-test suite that calls handle() directly. They show up only under a single-IP, multi-device load test run on a production-sized copy of the data.

Risks if ignored

  • **Critical (availability): the guest page and staff sign-in are rate-limited per IP behind venue

NAT.** Check-in halts after about 20 guests, and status polling is refused. Mitigation: event-specific limiter buckets keyed by event and verification attempt, not IP alone. Exempt CUSTOM APP's public routes from the global public-write bucket. Key status polling by status token. Raise or venue-allowlist auth/signin-fail for the event window, Super Administrator controlled and audited. Run the load test from one IP.

  • Critical (authorization): CORE admin = * is a hidden second Super Administrator.

Mitigation: event authority is event data, not a CORE permission.

  • event.superAdminUserId (exactly one) and delegations[] are checked by the module's own

eventCan(user, event, action, target).

  • CORE permission customapp.use only opens the module. CORE admins get no guest or table write

authority.

  • A CORE admin's only power is an audited "transfer Super Administrator" action (2-step required,

reason mandatory). The old holder loses authority in the same write. This is also the spec's "secure recovery without a second unrestricted account".

  • Critical (operations): an event-day restart wipes all staff sessions. Caused by any deploy,

including an accidental approval, or by a crash. Mitigation:

  • A technical freeze from 8 Dec 18:00 to 10 Dec: disable the workflow or add a required reviewer

with a wait timer. A promise is not a freeze.

  • Rehearse "restart at peak" and "re-login of all staff".
  • Guest status tokens must persist in the database, not in memory.
  • Persisting staff sessions across restarts is the better fix, but it changes CORE auth 12 days

out. Weigh it with Architect and Guardian.

  • **High (integrity): atomicity holds only if the critical section has no await, and only if

save failures are handled.** Mitigation:

  • One synchronous commit(event, plan): re-read state, check permissions and revs, check every

invariant on the candidate state, apply, db.save(). Email, file I/O and any await happen after the commit.

  • Idempotency key (clientOpId) on every mutation; a repeat returns the first result.
  • Per-guest and per-table rev; the client sends expectedRev, a mismatch returns 409 and

refreshes.

  • Measure production db.json size and save() time now. If it is over about 50 ms, consider

a separate data/customapp.json store. Offsite backup currently copies only db.json and the audit files, so that needs Operator work.

  • **High (privacy and spoofing): the surname + last-4 page can be probed and can check in someone

else.** Mitigation:

  • Identical response for no match, ambiguous match and locked-out: "Please see the registration

desk".

  • Per-event global failure cap plus a per-surname cap.
  • A random status token in the response (stored hashed). Never a guest id.
  • The status page shows first name, table and seat only.
  • A second self-check-in for an already-arrived guest flags staff; it does not silently show the

seat.

  • Registration activation toggle with an automatic time window.
  • High (scope): the spec's "Core" list is large for 12 days. If the acceptance scenarios are

not automated by about day 8, the event runs on untested transfer, swap and substitution paths. Mitigation: cut seat holds, seat numbers for non-preferred guests, SSE push and the graphical layout. Keep the invariant checker, polling with a revision counter, and the printable fallback.

Dependencies on other domains

  • Architect: event-scoped authority model (no CORE * bypass); the single commit(plan)

mutation path; per-record rev and clientOpId; decision on a separate data file versus shared db.json; polling with a since=rev change feed rather than SSE (SSE adds long-lived connections, a reconnect storm after restart, and session-touch questions, for little gain at 300 guests).

  • Operator: technical deploy freeze; measuring production db.json save time; venue network

(venue Wi-Fi plus a 4G backup router, and the egress IP known in advance); backup right before doors open; a restart-at-peak drill; offline packet printing and its custody.

  • Tuner: single-IP load model (300 guests polling plus 10 to 15 staff, with a save on each

mutation); poll intervals with jitter and backoff.

  • Guardian: PII in the hash-chained audit log cannot be purged later without breaking the

chain, so audit labels should use guest ref/id, not mobile or email. Also a post-event retention and purge policy, and control of printed lists.

  • Craftsman: all 12 acceptance scenarios as node --test interleaved handle() cases; a

property test that runs random operation sequences and asserts the invariants after each step; plus a Playwright multi-context journey.

  • Strategist: confirm the cut list (no holds; seat numbers for preferred guests only; no SSE)

with the client.

  • Scout: n8n lessons below are (unverified); please confirm with a real fetch if possible.

Residual risks (accepted)
  • Remote check-in with real knowledge of a guest's surname and phone digits. Accepted, because

arrival is confirmed physically at the desk or table and a duplicate attempt is flagged. Monitored through the Super Administrator's "repeat attempt" alerts.

  • One Super Administrator is a single point of failure. It is a spec requirement. Accepted,

with a rehearsed CORE-admin transfer procedure (target under 2 minutes) and the Super Administrator's ability to reallocate queues in bulk.

  • Restart loses staff sessions (if sessions are not persisted). Accepted only with a tested

freeze and a rehearsed re-login. Monitored by uptime checks on the event day.

Appendix C: Invariants (assert after every commit, in tests and in production)

Checked on the candidate state inside commit(). If any fails, the commit throws before db.save(), so in-memory state is never mutated unless it is valid (build a plan, validate, then apply).

  1. For every active table, confirmed assignments + active reservations (+ holds, if any) <= capacity <= 10.
  2. A guest has at most one active assignment or reservation. A seat has at most one occupant.
  3. Original and substitute never both consume a seat.
  4. Every guest and every table has at most one owner. There is exactly one Super Administrator per

event.

  1. A checked-in guest is in exactly one of three states: seated, in a queue (owner or Super

Administrator), or referred.

  1. Seated implies the guest is eligible for that table's classification (unless the Super

Administrator override is recorded).

Expose GET /api/customapp/events/:id/health to the Super Administrator. It runs the invariants on demand and shows any violation in red on the dashboard, a last layer of the Swiss cheese.

db.json write failure (disk full)Mutation lost after restartCheck free disk in preflight; save() throwing returns 500 to the client (no false success).
Load test run against productionERP users disrupted; data pollutedLoad test on a restored copy of production data on a separate instance or port.
SMTP provider throttle (for example about 30 per minute)Delayed emailsOutbox with concurrency 1–2 and backoff; the on-screen display is the primary channel.
Event-day code fix neededRestart during the eventA Super Administrator-reachable config toggle instead of code; hotfix only in a lull, with announcement.
StrategistComplete
View position

Strategist Position: CUSTOM APP and the YEP 2026 Registration & Table Assignment app

Lens: gold. Value per unit of effort, MVP cut line, sequencing against a fixed date. Grounding: spec.txt (sections 1 to 22), interview summary, codebase map, Architect/Advocate/Skeptic round 1 drafts, and direct checks of the repo: server/api.js:558-562,1445-1447 (anon and public-write buckets), docs/guides/ + test/guides.test.js (CORE has the same guides gate as ATLAS), and the release cadence in git log (Document Management, MOD-08, went from engine to release in about 3 days, 22 to 24 Nov; server/documents.js is 943 lines, server/forms.js 477). n8n: not present locally; n8n references here are from model knowledge and are marked (unverified).

Core recommendation

Treat 9 December as an immovable launch, not a target. Build in three tiers so that CORE is event-ready at the end of every tier, and the event can still run if the next tier slips:

  • Tier 0, staff-only seating: done 1 Dec.
  • Tier 1, guest QR self-check-in and delegated queues: done 3 Dec.
  • Tier 2, groups, substitutions, email and dashboard: done 5 Dec, with the acceptance demo.

The last four days (6 to 9 December) are for load testing, a dress rehearsal on the real guest list, fixes only, and the deploy freeze. Make it "reusable" by parameterising (every record keyed by eventId, classifications and table types as data, no YEP strings in code), never by abstracting (no app builder, no plugin system, no generic outbox). Cut SSE/WebSocket, seat holds, the floor plan, SMS and analytics.

Key argument

The spec says it plainly in section 17: "Prioritize a dependable workflow over elaborate features." Section 22 adds: "Optional enhancements should not delay the core event-day workflow." The value of this system is concentrated in one loop:

> verified guest → a staffer picks an eligible table → capacity holds → the guest sees the table number

Everything else either speeds up that loop (QR self-check-in, recommendations, groups) or makes it safe to change afterwards (transfers, substitutions, audit, email). So the build order is:

  1. The loop, operated by staff alone. Import, assisted search, manual check-in, preferred

retrieval, choose table, capacity enforced in one synchronous commit(), CSV export and a printable seating plan.

  1. Taking work off the desk. The guest QR page, plus the per-owner queues with ranked

recommendations.

  1. The exception paths that the acceptance scenarios name.

Staff-assisted check-in is a hard requirement in section 4 anyway, so Tier 0 is never wasted work. It is also the fallback if the public page misbehaves on venue Wi-Fi.

The biggest schedule risk is not code. CORE ships a module this size in about 3 days: MOD-08 went from engine to release between 22 and 24 Nov. The real risk is the client's inputs, which section 22 still lists as open:

  • the final guest list with industries,
  • industry relationships,
  • the table inventory and which tables are mixed,
  • whether seat numbers are needed for non-preferred guests,
  • the staff roster and delegation,
  • whether no-show release can be delegated.

Each of these needs a dated deadline, so the engineering plan cannot be blocked by them.

One architectural choice saves the most effort. All mutations (check-in, assign, transfer, swap, release, group assign, substitute, reallocate) go through one validate-apply-save function with guest revisions. Then transfers, swaps, groups and substitutions become thin commands of size S, not M. It also gives the "last seat" and "stale write" acceptance scenarios one test surface instead of eight. That single function is the strategic bet that makes the 12 days fit.

Live sync by short or long polling on an event sequence number (since=seq) is enough for ~15 staff devices and ~300 guest status pages. A push channel in the style of n8n's SSE/WS (unverified) is new infrastructure in server.js, carries risk on the day, and adds nothing a 2 to 3 second poll cannot give. It is "Won't (this time)".

Reusability is worth about 5% extra effort, and no more. CUSTOM APP becomes:

  • a module entry (shared/modules.js),
  • a landing page that lists client apps (one app today),
  • an events collection.

YEP 2026 is just event #1. The second event or client app reuses the engine. Anything beyond that is speculative; we decide it after 9 Dec, using real usage data.

Risks if ignored

  • Building the elaborate parts first and running out of days. If the push channel, floor plan,

seat picker or a generic "custom app framework" are built before the staff loop works end-to-end, the event could arrive with nothing dependable. The tiering exists so that a slip costs us a feature, never the event.

  • A tested system with the wrong data. If the guest list, industry mapping and table inventory

arrive on 8 Dec, the recommendations will be wrong, preferred seats will be missing, and there will be no time for a dress rehearsal. The mitigation is client deadlines: decisions by 1 Dec, final data by 3 Dec, data freeze by 6 Dec. After 6 Dec, changes go through the app UI only, with audit.

  • Launch blockers in CORE's existing safeguards, which no feature work will fix. Skeptic and

Advocate both found them, and I confirmed them in server/api.js:562,1447:

  • public-write allows 20 per hour per IP and api-anon 120 per minute per IP. Behind the

venue's shared NAT, guest 21 is refused.

  • A CORE admin holds *, which would make every CORE admin a second Super Administrator.
  • Sessions are held in memory and are lost on any restart.

These are XS to S fixes, but they belong in Tier 0, not at the end. They are cheap now and fatal on the day.

Dependencies on other domains

  • Architect: confirm that one synchronous commit() (no await between validate and

db.save()) covers every mutation. The S-sizing of transfer, swap, group and substitution depends on it. Confirm the data model (seat embedded in the guest, a single owner field).

  • Advocate: the minimum screen set for Tier 0 and Tier 1:
  • "My queue" on a phone,
  • "Event control" on a tablet or desktop,
  • the guest page plus the status-token page.

Also the recovery screens for a 409 refusal. These are Must, because a refusal without options breaks the loop.

  • Skeptic / Guardian: the threat-model bar for the public page. Proposed: surname + last 4

digits, uniform responses, a registration on/off toggle, and event-scoped rate-limit buckets sized for one NAT. Also the Super Administrator break-glass procedure: a CORE admin reassigns superAdminUserId, audited, with no second active account.

  • Operator:
  • the freeze mechanism (disable the deploy workflow or require approval from 8 to 10 Dec),
  • a backup and restore drill on 7 Dec,
  • whether sessions survive a restart, or we accept that risk under the freeze,
  • the printed contingency pack.
  • Craftsman: the 12 acceptance scenarios as node --test cases against handle(), built from

day 1 alongside each tier. Also one Playwright multi-device journey and a load script: 300 guests plus 15 staff in a 10-minute burst.

  • Tuner: whole-file db.save() cost under the arrival burst, with the shared db.json

including other CORE modules. Does save coalescing or a separate event data file need to be in Tier 0?

  • Client (Legati/ICD), through the user: the six section-22 decisions by 1 Dec, the final

data by 3 Dec, the staff roster with CORE accounts by 4 Dec, and the dress rehearsal on 6 or 7 Dec.

Appendix C: MVP feature set and cut line

  • [ ] Tier 0 (#1 to #10, #24, first AS tests): about 5 to 6 days of work, compressed to about 3

calendar days at CORE cadence

  • [ ] Tier 1 (#11 to #15, #25, #28): about 4 days of work, about 2 calendar days
  • [ ] Tier 2 (#16 to #23): about 6 days of work, about 2 calendar days
  • Total: about 15 days of work at T-shirt sizing, against 9 build days. This fits only

with CORE's demonstrated AI-assisted cadence (MOD-08 took about 3 days) and the commit() leverage. Buffer: 6 to 8 Dec.

  • Cut line rationale: everything named in the spec's section 19 "Core" list, or in a section 20

acceptance scenario, is above the line. Everything in section 19 "Optional" is below it. So is infrastructure that a poll or a single-thread commit makes unnecessary (push, holds, workers).

  • Fallback order if we slip: drop #19 email to "display + verbal" (spec section 6 allows

verbal communication, and the AS "Email failure" row requires the flow to work without email), then simplify #20 dashboard to counts + occupancy list, then make #17 groups "assign one by one with a capacity hint". Never drop #2, #7, #12, #13 or #21.

Appendix E: Success metrics (light analytics-design, from timestamps already in the change log)

Every guest record already carries verifiedAt, checkedInAt and seatedAt, and emails carry notifiedAt. These come from the change log at no extra cost, so we can measure:

  • Zero overbooked tables, duplicate check-ins or double seats. This is a hard gate, checked by

an invariant scan after the load test and after the event.

  • QR scan to "checked in": median under 30 s.
  • Checked in to seated (regular/employee): 90th percentile under 2 minutes at peak.
  • Peak queue depth per staffer, and the share of guests seated at a recommended table (does the

ranking help?).

  • Email delivered / failed / resent counts.
  • Share of guests who self-verified versus were assisted (does the QR page pay for itself?). This

answers the post-event decision on investing in the guest page for event #2.

ScoutComplete
View position

Scout Position — CUSTOM APP / YEP 2026 Registration & Table Assignment

Core recommendation: Copy what ticketing and check-in products already do, and use n8n only for its transport and public-form patterns. From pretix, take the write contract: an idempotency nonce on every write, structured reason codes on refusal, capacity counted as confirmed + reserved + held, and a lock taken before the availability check. For offline work, take pretix's upload-later model with the original timestamp. From n8n, take the waiting-form status page (an opaque token compared in constant time, with the page polling a status suffix), the SSE wire format, and the in-memory capacity queue, but only to throttle the email outbox. Reject n8n queue mode and its multi-main pub/sub relay; they show why CORE must stay single-process. Separately, before building anything public, fix one blocker found in CORE itself: today's per-IP limits (public-write 20/hour, api-anon 120/min) would lock out the whole guest crowd once they share the venue Wi-Fi's single address.

Key argument: Mature event products have already solved the hard parts of this brief. The pretix check-in redeem API takes an optional nonce so that "sending the same nonce again succeeds but creates only one check-in", which allows safe retries after a dropped connection. It refuses with machine-readable reasons (already_redeemed, ambiguous when several tickets match, blocked, unapproved). It accepts force plus a historical datetime, used "to upload offline scans that already happened" (pretix check-in API). That one contract covers three of the twelve acceptance scenarios: duplicate/simultaneous check-in, the last available seat (a refusal with a reason makes the losing device refresh), and connectivity/recovery. pretix counts capacity as orders paid or within their payment period plus non-expired cart positions, and it reports pending orders, cart positions and blocking vouchers as separate figures (pretix quotas). That is the spec's section 8 rule (confirmed + active reservations + holds count toward 10). pretix takes a lock only for actions that make a resource less available, takes it before the availability check, and needs none to free capacity (pretix locking). In CORE the synchronous handler plays the lock, and the rule becomes "no await between check and db.save()". Cvent OnArrival's kiosk lets planners limit which fields attendees can search, so attendees cannot browse other registrants' names (Skift review). This supports the interview decision: surname + last 4 digits, one exact match or go to staff, never a candidate list. n8n was checked against its real source (a sparse clone at commit bafcff7, 27 Nov 2026; the session's codebase map assumed it was unavailable). Its waiting-form flow is the guest status page we need. waiting-webhooks.ts checks the length, then compares the opaque resumeToken with timingSafeEqual, and a bad token gets the same 401 page. waiting-forms.ts serves a status suffix that the page polls until the run completes. n8n keeps HMAC-signed URLs for the case where query parameters such as approved=true must be tamper-proof, which we do not need. CORE already does the token half: server/helpdesk.js:518 issues a randomBytes(24) publicToken. n8n's SSE backend (packages/cli/src/push/sse.push.ts, 56 lines) shows that server push on raw Node needs only a few headers (text/event-stream, X-Accel-Buffering: no), a :ok primer, a 60-second ping, and one connection per (user, tab) that replaces the old one. It sits behind auth middleware with allowSkipMFA: false. Because EventSource sends no Origin, the origin check accepts Sec-Fetch-Site: same-origin instead. So SSE is a cheap upgrade if long-polling proves too slow. It is not a prerequisite. The message should stay a thin "event revision N changed" signal, and clients re-fetch a delta with since=N. n8n's concurrency-queue.ts decrements a capacity counter synchronously and parks the overflow in a FIFO queue of promises. That pattern fits the SMTP outbox, so 300 confirmation emails cannot pile onto the helpdesk mail server at once. It does not fit seat allocation: the spec says the losing request refreshes or escalates, so seat requests must fail fast, never wait in a queue. No surveyed product recommends tables by industry affinity at arrival. zkipster's "autoseat" fills the available seats, which is the sequential fill the spec forbids (zkipster seating). Social Tables-style tools are planner drag-and-drop. This is the one area to build from scratch, so keep it deterministic and explainable: a score made of tiers with "why" chips, not a black box.

Risks if ignored:

  • Guests locked out by rate limits on event night. server/api.js:562,1447 applies public-write (20 per hour per IP) to every non-GET /api/public/* call, and api-anon (120 per minute per IP) to every anonymous call. Behind venue Wi-Fi NAT, or carrier CGNAT for guests on mobile data, the 21st guest check-in in an hour from that address gets "Too many messages from this network". A guest status page polling every 4 seconds uses up api-anon with only about 8 phones. The event paths need their own buckets: per-token for status polls, per-IP with a ceiling sized for a crowd of 300 behind one address, per-surname for failed verifications, and an event-wide brake on failures. They must be exempt from the generic public-write. Test this in the load test from a single source IP.
  • Retries that double-book or double-check-in. Without a client-generated idempotency key (pretix's nonce) on check-in, assign, transfer and swap, a tap repeated over flaky venue Wi-Fi becomes two writes. Offline devices that each allocate independently produce duplicates: FooEvents and Ticketsolve warn that the same ticket can be accepted by two offline devices (FooEvents, Ticketsolve). Keep the spec's "single allocation authority or pre-partitioned ranges" and reconcile with nonce + original timestamp.
  • Reinventing, or adding infrastructure. Copying n8n's queue mode (Bull/Redis) or its multi-main pub/sub relay (push/index.ts shouldRelayViaPubSub) adds a process boundary. That breaks the one guarantee that makes "only one gets the last seat" trivial in CORE. Skipping what CORE already ships (public/js/vendor/qrcode.js for the reception QR, shared/csv.js parseCsv, server/pdf/office-core.js readZip/parseXml for .xlsx import, server/mail sendMail, the helpdesk public-token flow, pagePolicy CSP hashes) wastes some of the 12 days.

Dependencies on other domains:

  • Architect: commits to a per-event revision counter and a since delta feed, which both long-poll and optional SSE ride on, plus an opId/nonce dedupe store per event (pretix-style). This decides whether SSE is a 60-line add-on or a redesign.
  • Skeptic/Guardian: the rate-limit bucket design and keys (token vs IP vs surname), the verification-failure lockout, and whether the status token is shown only once or can be sent again by email.
  • Operator: confirms the Caddy reverse-proxy config does not buffer streamed or long-held responses (Caddy is believed to flush text/event-stream automatically; verify in deploy/aws), knows the venue network topology (one public IP or not), and gets the paper fallback printed.
  • Strategist: keeps the recommendation engine to a simple tiered score for MVP, with affinity/exclusion tags optional, as spec section 19 already allows.
  • Craftsman: turns pretix-style reason codes into assertions in the 12 acceptance scenarios, and adds a single-source-IP load test.

UX approach summary
AspectpretixCvent OnArrivalzkipster
OnboardingImport/order data; device paired to a check-in listEvent preloaded; kiosk locked so only authorized users can exitSpreadsheet import with table/seat columns
Core interactionScan → green/yellow ("already used")/redAttendee searches self → confirm → badgeTap guest → see or reseat
Info architectureCheck-in list = scopeKiosk vs staff appGuest list + seating tab
Visual styleFull-screen colour verdictBranded kioskClean iPad list
Differentiation opportunities
  1. Explainable affinity ranking. No surveyed product ranks tables by industry at arrival. Show tiers (same industry > related > other eligible) with capacity and the "why" chips staff need to choose quickly.
  2. Ownership-scoped queues. Check-in tools scope by device or list. The spec needs exclusive per-person guest and table ownership with instant reallocation. Model it as data (owner fields re-read on every write), not as UI.
  3. Pending-table status page. Most tools stop at "checked in". The n8n waiting-page pattern gives the guest a live "waiting for your table" state, then table/seat, at no extra infrastructure cost.
Lessons from prior art
  • Adopt: idempotency nonce + reason codes (pretix); capacity = confirmed + reserved + holds (pretix quotas); lock before the availability check, none to free (pretix locking); restricted self-search fields (Cvent); "already used" shown in yellow rather than as an error (pretixSCAN); opaque status token + polled status endpoint (n8n waiting forms).
  • Avoid: independent offline allocation on several devices (duplicate risk noted by FooEvents/Ticketsolve); sequential autoseat (zkipster; forbidden by the spec); queue/multi-process scaling for a 300-guest event (n8n queue mode, pub/sub relay); per-IP-only rate limits on a public crowd endpoint (CORE today).
  • Innovate: the industry-affinity recommendation and group fit. No prior art found, so keep it deterministic and testable.
