Operator Position: YEP 2026 Registration & Table Assignment on CORE production

Lens: orange. Deploy, monitor, roll back, fail at 2x load. Grounding: spec.txt (sections 17, 18, 19 and 20), interview summary, codebase map, the round 1 drafts from Architect, Advocate, Skeptic and Strategist, and direct reads of:

  • .github/workflows/deploy-ec2.yml
  • deploy/aws/{update.sh, remote-deploy.sh, ltdc-erp.service, ltdc-erp-backup.cron, ltdc-erp-offsite.timer, ltdc-erp-restore-check.timer, Caddyfile, EC2.md}
  • server/db.js (save), server/auth.js:54 (createSessions), server/api.js:554-570 and 1440-1447 (rate limits), server/offsite.js (manifest file names), server.js:262-263 (timeouts), server/monitor-panel.js:389 (health)

n8n: this workspace has no local clone (the codebase map says so, and find finds none). Every n8n pattern below comes from model knowledge and is marked (unverified).

Core recommendation

Treat 9 December as a production incident we already know is coming. CORE runs on one t3.micro with one Node process, and staff sessions are held only in memory. That means any restart, whether from a deploy, a crash or an OOM, signs every staffer out at the peak of arrivals. So the operational plan matters as much as the code:

  • a deploy freeze enforced in two places, not just agreed;
  • a kill switch for CUSTOM APP, plus runtime knobs, so nothing on the night needs a deploy;
  • a server sized up for the evening, and a load test on a prod-sized staging copy;
  • event snapshots every 5 minutes, because the hourly backups are far too coarse for an evening of check-ins;
  • a rehearsed degrade path: self check-in, then staff-only, then paper, then reconciliation.

Key argument

What can hurt us on the night is not the feature code. It is how CORE gets deployed, restarted and restored:

  1. Every push deploys, and a deploy is a restart. Every push to claude/busy-curie-hfpbg0 deploys once tests and journeys pass (deploy-ec2.yml). The production environment waits for approval only if required reviewers are set. update.sh runs systemctl stop, then rsync --delete, then start. That is several seconds of downtime, and with createSessions keeping a Map in memory, every session is lost.
  2. Rollback is slow. It means a git revert and push, then the full CI run (about 10-20 min), then a deploy that is itself another restart. No copy of the previous release is kept on the box.
  3. Restoring data restores all of CORE. The restore path (RESTORE.md section 1) goes back to an hourly snapshot of the whole db.json. Restoring to 18:00 at 19:00 throws away an hour of check-ins and seat assignments, plus every other module's changes.
  4. Crashes are fatal. I found no unhandledRejection handler. On Node 22, one rejected promise left unhandled in a fire-and-forget email send ends the process. Restart=always, RestartSec=3 brings it back, but signed out.
  5. The server is small. A t3.micro (1 GB, burstable) rewrites the whole of CORE's pretty-printed JSON on every mutation, on the same process that holds about 300 guest connections polling or long-polling. Nobody has measured production's db.json size or how long save() takes.

My answers, in the same order:

  1. Freeze. Turn on required reviewers for the production GitHub environment from 7 Dec 18:00 (a settings change, no code). Ship a server-side freeze file now: remote-deploy.sh refuses when /opt/ltdc-erp/data/DEPLOY_FREEZE exists, unless the deploy carries an override.
  2. Fast rollback. Make update.sh keep /opt/ltdc-erp-prev and add a one-line ltdc-erp-rollback, so rolling back takes under a minute without going through CI.
  3. Kill switches and knobs that need no deploy. These are the Admin panel → Modules switch for customapp, plus per-event toggles stored in data:
  4. registrationOpen: the guest page;
  5. selfCheckIn: off means staff-only assisted check-in;
  6. emailPaused: holds the outbox;
  7. pollMs: the server tells clients how often to poll, so we can slow 300 phones down without shipping code.
  8. Event-grade recovery. Architect's per-event change log with seq is also our recovery journal. Every 5 minutes during the event window, write an event-only snapshot (event, tables, guests, log) to data/backups/events/. The command-desk laptop also downloads the event export every 5 minutes. That copy survives losing the instance and losing the venue network, and it feeds the printed pack.
  9. Process hygiene. Every async path in the module (outbox, notifications) is wrapped and tested. Add a process-level unhandledRejection logger that records the error and does not exit. Long-polls must stay under server.requestTimeout (120 s). I recommend 25 s with a heartbeat, and they must be excluded from the Security monitor's latency figures.
  10. Capacity. Resize to t3.small or t3.medium on 7 Dec. That is a stop/start of about 2 minutes, done before the freeze, at roughly US$0.02-0.04 an hour extra. Resize back on 10 Dec.

Staging: one spin-up, three uses. Build a temporary staging server from the hourly off-server copy using RESTORE.md section 2. It will:

  • prove disaster recovery works for real, 12 days before we might need it;
  • give the load test production-sized data (the real db.json, so the real save() cost);
  • stay up on 9 Dec as a warm standby on its own hostname with its certificate already issued, so a dead primary means "restore the latest event snapshot to standby, give staff the standby URL, switch the QR redirect".

**What we take from n8n (unverified):**

  • Its concurrency-limited execution queue (concurrency-control) becomes our outbox: at most N SMTP sends in flight, retry with backoff, status per message.
  • The idea of decoupling the HTTP response from the work (Form Trigger onReceived) means check-in answers at once and email goes out afterwards.
  • We explicitly reject queue mode (Bull/Redis workers). A second process destroys the single-thread atomicity, and it adds infrastructure that can fail at 3 AM.

Monitoring. People will be in the room, so we do not need PagerDuty. We need:

  • an external 1-minute uptime check on /api/public/health;
  • an event health panel showing save duration p95, the event seq, open polls, oldest pending guest's wait, outbox depth and failures, and 4xx/429 counts on the event routes;
  • an on-call developer at the venue, or on the phone, holding the rollback, restore and standby runbooks.

Risks if ignored

  • Restart at peak equals total outage for staff. A routine push on the 9th, or one unhandled rejection in the email path, restarts the process. Every staffer is signed out and has to sign in again with password and 2-step. On shared venue Wi-Fi, about 15 staff re-authenticating trips the auth bucket (20/min/IP) and signin-fail (per address). The queue stops right when 40 guests are at the door.
  • The recovery point is an hour, and the only rollback is CORE-wide. Without event-scoped snapshots and a replayable log, recovering from a bad write or a lost instance means picking between losing an hour of seating and hand-entering everything again. Doing a whole-db.json restore mid-event also rolls back HR, CRM and projects for the whole company.
  • Capacity is unmeasured on a 1 GB burstable box. If production's db.json is tens of MB, JSON.stringify(data, null, 2) on every check-in, combined with 300 polling phones, can push event-loop latency past what staff will tolerate. The existing per-IP limits (public-write 20/h, api-anon 120/min) will start returning 429s to real guests on the venue NAT. All of this must be found in a load test on staging, not at 18:40 on the 9th.

Dependencies on other domains

  • Architect: a per-event seq change log that can be replayed as commands (offline reconciliation and snapshot restore both use it). An event-only export and import that restores one event without touching the rest of db.json. Runtime toggles kept in event data, not in code. Long-poll timeout under 120 s with server-supplied pollMs. Email kept outside commit().
  • Skeptic / Guardian: event rate-limit buckets sized for one shared NAT address, which need numbers and the load test. A decision on whether sessions survive a restart (persisting hashed session tokens) or we accept re-sign-in and raise auth limits for the event window. How long PII is kept in backups: the nightly copies (30 days) and the S3 offsite copies will hold guest PII after any in-app purge.
  • Tuner: a measured save() cost on production-sized data, a poll-interval budget, and the load-test profile (40 arrivals in 10 min, 300 guest pages, about 15 staff devices, a last-seat race, all from one IP).
  • Craftsman: a stdlib-only load script (scripts/event-load.mjs), a multi-context Playwright journey for the last seat, and a "kill the process during arrivals" chaos test on staging.
  • Strategist: a hard date for code freeze (7 Dec 18:00) and deploy freeze (8 Dec 12:00 to 10 Dec 09:00), with no feature work after 6 Dec.
  • Client / ICD: the SMTP provider's sending limits and SPF/DKIM for the From address; the venue Wi-Fi details and a 4G hotspot for staff (a separate IP from guests); printers and power at the registration desk; confirmation of the final guest list by 7 Dec.

Deployment Strategy

Method: a single-instance restart deploy (as today) with features dark behind flags. Blue-green and canary are not worth building in 12 days on one box. Risk is contained by:

  1. Shipping the module dark: CUSTOM APP is off in Admin panel → Modules until the event is configured.
  2. Changing data additively only (||= defaults, new collections).
  3. Keeping a previous-release copy so rollback takes under 1 minute.
  4. Freezing early.

Rollback speed target: under 60 s for code (ltdc-erp-rollback) and under 5 min for event data (restore the event snapshot).

Rollback Checklist
  • [ ] Trigger: 5xx on event routes more than 1% for 2 minutes; health check failing for 2 consecutive minutes; save() p95 above 250 ms; a capacity invariant violated (any table over 10, or one guest holding two seats); event-loop stalls reported by staff.
  • [ ] First lever (no restart): set selfCheckIn=false (staff-only), emailPaused=true, raise pollMs. If the module itself is faulty, turn CUSTOM APP off in Admin panel → Modules. The rest of CORE keeps running.
  • [ ] Code rollback: sudo ltdc-erp-rollback swaps in /opt/ltdc-erp-prev and restarts. If it is not available: git revert <sha> && git push, then approve the deploy (about 15 min). Both restart the process and sign staff out, so announce "sign in again" to staff first.
  • [ ] Event data rollback: stop staff writes (selfCheckIn=false and an event readOnly=true), then restore the most recent good data/backups/events/<event>-<HHMM>.json through the event import (one event only). Replay the change log after that point, or re-enter it from paper. Never restore the whole db.json during the event unless the instance itself is lost.
  • [ ] Instance lost: restore the latest event snapshot from the command-desk laptop onto the warm standby. Staff switch to the standby URL. The QR destination (a short redirect we control) is pointed at standby. Reconcile into production after the event.
  • [ ] Verify: /api/public/health is OK; the event health panel shows seq advancing; the capacity self-check passes (no table over 10, no duplicate seats); one test check-in on the rehearsal guest goes through.
  • [ ] Notify: command desk (Super Administrator) by voice and radio, then the staff group chat: "Use paper sheets for your tables until told otherwise." Post-mortem by 11 Dec.

Appendix B: Event-day runbook (T-3h to close)

T-3h (15:00)

  1. DEPLOY_FREEZE exists; the GitHub reviewer gate is on.
  2. Run an off-site backup by hand and confirm it in the Security monitor.
  3. The latest event snapshot is under 5 minutes old; the laptop has downloaded the export.
  4. Smoke-test the rehearsal guest end to end.
  5. Print the event pack:
  6. A to Z guest list with classification, owner and preferred table and seat;
  7. one sheet per table;
  8. one sheet per staffer (their delegated guests and tables form their pre-partitioned offline range);
  9. blank reconciliation sheets.

Mark each page "as of HH:MM".

  1. Check every staff device: each one is signed in, uses the 4G hotspot (not guest Wi-Fi), is charged, and has a power bank.
  2. Fire a test email and confirm it arrives.

Doors

  • Set registrationOpen=true.
  • Watch the event health panel.
  • The on-call developer sits next to the Super Administrator.

Degrade ladder:

  1. Raise pollMs.
  2. Pause email.
  3. Turn self check-in off (staff-only).
  4. Go to paper by partition.
  5. Fail over to standby.

Reconciliation:

  • Turn on readOnly.
  • The SA enters paper allocations in owner order through the normal commands. Capacity and duplicate checks still apply, and conflicts go to the SA queue.
  • Export, compare against the paper totals, then turn readOnly off.

Close

  • Set registrationOpen=false.
  • Take the final export (CSV) and a final snapshot.
  • Run an off-site backup by hand.
  • Put a retention date on the event record.
CraftsmanComplete
View position

Craftsman Position: CUSTOM APP / YEP 2026 Registration & Table Assignment

Lens: purple. Testability, patterns, regression protection, and the developer who changes this code on 7 December with guests arriving in 48 hours.

Grounding: spec.txt (sections 1 to 22, the 12 acceptance scenarios in section 07), the codebase map, round 1 drafts from Architect, Advocate, Scout, Skeptic and Strategist, and these direct checks of the repository:

  • node --test: 716 tests, 3 min 10 s on this machine (measured today).
  • server/api.js:1433-1460: handle() has exactly one await, the one on the handler. A

synchronous handler is therefore one turn of the event loop.

  • server/util.js:15: HttpError(status, message) carries no structured payload.
  • scripts/journeys.mjs:43: the browser journeys spawn CORE with RATE_LIMITS: 'off'.
  • .github/workflows/deploy-ec2.yml: test + journeys must pass before deploy.
  • test/guides.test.js:20: a hard-coded MODULES list; every sidebar label needs a guide.
  • test/crm.test.js, test/public-form.test.js: the in-process client() / visitor(ip) harness.
  • Only two test files use Promise.all, and both use it for logins, not for races.

On n8n: the clone that Architect and Scout read is no longer on disk. The n8n testing notes below are from model knowledge and marked (unverified). The n8n runtime patterns I build on are the ones Architect and Scout verified.

Core recommendation

Make the seating engine a pure, synchronous plan(state, command, actor, now) function, wrapped by one commit() that applies the plan and saves. Guard it with one shared checkInvariants() that runs:

  • after every unit test,
  • inside a seeded random-command fuzz test,
  • at the end of the load script, and
  • in production, as a read-only health check.

Then turn the spec's 12 acceptance scenarios into 12 named node --test cases, plus one multi-device browser journey. The client gets the test output as the acceptance evidence, and every fix in the last four days is re-proved automatically before it can deploy.

Key argument

The spec's hardest guarantees are the ones a normal test suite here cannot see:

  • the last seat goes to exactly one request,
  • a check-in is never duplicated,
  • a transfer or swap is atomic,
  • a stale write is refused.

CORE's in-process harness calls handle() one request at a time. Promise.all over two "assign" calls, with bodies already parsed, runs them sequentially. The test goes green whether or not the handler is atomic.

The atomicity rests on a fact about structure, not behavior: no await between the check and db.save(). The first person to break it will be well-meaning. Someone adds an await sendMail() or an await audit... inside the confirm handler on 6 December to "email the assignment right away". All 716 tests still pass, and the last-seat race opens in production. So the guarantee has to be built into the structure and tested at the structure:

  1. Mutations register through a mutation(route, ...) helper that **refuses an AsyncFunction

handler at startup**, and a test asserts this.

  1. The pure plan() is exercised directly, in milliseconds, with no database file and an injected

clock.

  1. A real-socket race test spawns server.js and fires N parallel fetch calls at the last seat.

The same discipline answers "reusable without over-engineering". The engine is reusable when a second fixture event passes the same suite: different capacity, no employee tables, no seat numbers. That proves no YEP strings are hard-coded, which a plugin framework would not prove. The Rule of Three says build no "CUSTOM APP SDK" until the third client app.

What does deserve abstraction now, because it would otherwise be written in 8 places:

  • the event fixture builder,
  • the refusal-code catalogue, and
  • checkInvariants().

Each of those will be repeated by every one of the 8 mutation types (check in, assign, transfer, swap, release, group assign, substitute, reallocate) and every one of the 5 or more test files.

The design this lens asks of the build (concrete, CORE-idiomatic)
shared/customapp/
  refusals.js     // frozen catalogue shared by server + browser: STALE, FULL, SEAT_TAKEN,
                  // OUT_OF_SCOPE, NOT_ELIGIBLE, ALREADY_CHECKED_IN, REGISTRATION_CLOSED,
                  // DUPLICATE_NONCE_DIFFERENT_BODY, NOT_SUPER_ADMIN, TABLE_HAS_ASSIGNMENTS
  rank.js         // pure recommendation ranking (same / related industry, capacity, group fit)
  invariants.js   // checkInvariants(eventState) -> [] | [{rule, ids, detail}]
server/customapp/
  engine.js       // plan(state, cmd, actor, now) -> {ok:true, changes, events} | {ok:false, code, fresh}
                  // NO db, NO Date.now(), NO async, NO I/O. Pure.
  commit.js       // commit(db, cmd, actor): nonce check -> plan -> apply -> seq++ -> db.save()
                  // synchronous; then returns after-effects (email, wake pollers) for the caller
                  // to run AFTER the save
  routes.js       // registerCustomAppRoutes(route, db, {audit}); every write via mutation()
  public.js       // /api/public/event/:code/* (verify, status token)
  outbox.js       // email queue + per-message status; async, outside commit

Two small changes to CORE's shared code, each with its own test:

  • HttpError gets an optional third argument, new HttpError(409, msg, { code, fresh }), and

handle() serializes it as { error, code, fresh }. This is backward compatible: no existing caller passes a third argument. The losing device then gets typed fresh options, which is what the Advocate's recovery screens need. Tests assert code, never message text, so copy can be improved for staff without breaking 40 assertions.

  • mutation() wrapper:
// server/customapp/routes.js
function mutation(route, method, path, perm, handler) {
  if (handler.constructor.name === 'AsyncFunction')
    throw new Error(`${method} ${path}: event writes must be synchronous (no await before save)`);
  route(method, path, perm, handler);
}

Risks if ignored

  • Silent loss of atomicity (regression risk, highest). The in-process suite cannot detect an

await added to the critical section, and the browser journeys cannot either. They run with rate limits off and one clicker per page. The last-seat and duplicate-check-in guarantees would rest on code review alone, during the most rushed week of the project.

  • The #1 launch blocker is invisible to CI as it stands. Skeptic, Scout and Advocate all found

that public-write is 20 per hour per IP and api-anon is 120 per minute per IP. With 300 guests behind venue NAT, that limit is hit. journeys.mjs sets RATE_LIMITS: 'off', so even a perfect browser journey passes. The guest path needs an in-process test with limits ON: 300 verifies plus polling from one x-forwarded-for, as public-form.test.js already does with TRUST_PROXY=1.

  • Pattern drift across 8 mutation types (consistency risk). If each handler does its own

"count seats", one of them will forget that active reservations and holds count toward 10 (spec section 8). Swap and transfer are the usual culprits. One plan() with one capacity function, checked by one checkInvariants() after every fuzzed step, makes that bug impossible to merge.

  • Velocity collapse in the last 4 days (DX risk). The full suite takes 3 min 10 s and journeys

run on top of that. If the event tests are not runnable alone in under 5 s (node --test test/customapp-*.test.js), developers will skip them while fixing at the venue rehearsal.

  • Guides gate surprise (process risk). CORE's test/guides.test.js hard-codes MODULES and

requires a guide for every sidebar label. Adding the CUSTOM APP sidebar without a docs/guides/events.md (roles: event staff, admin) and a whats-new.md line fails npm test, which blocks deploy. The spec also asks for a "brief operator guide" (section 20). Write it once, as the guide, and it satisfies both.

Dependencies on other domains

  • Architect: split commit() into a pure plan() and an apply/save step. Give refusals the

typed codes listed above. Return after-effects from commit(), never run them inside it. Agree the seq / long-poll contract so a test can assert "device B sees change within one poll".

  • Prover: the authoritative invariant list for checkInvariants(). My draft is in Appendix C.
  • Skeptic / Guardian: the abuse cases, as concrete test rows (enumeration timing, wrong last-4

digits, token replay, master list never in a public response, PII fields in exports and staff read-only views).

  • Tuner: the load targets: guests per minute at peak, staff count, poll interval, acceptable

p95, and db.save() cost at the real file size, so scripts/event-load.mjs has pass/fail thresholds, not just numbers.

  • Operator: a prod-like rehearsal target (a restored copy of production db.json) for the load

script. A tagged freeze commit with test + journeys green. The freeze mechanism itself (environment reviewer) is theirs.

  • Advocate: the recovery screens ("Table 5 just filled. Updated options:"), so the journey asserts

them by visible text and the screenshot script captures them for the guide.

  • Strategist: test scope per tier. The acceptance cases for a tier must be green at that tier's

date (Appendix E).

Appendix B: Test pyramid summary (testing-strategy Steps 3 and 6)

LayerFilesApprox. countRun timeMock strategy
Unit (pure)customapp-engine, customapp-rank, customapp-invariants, customapp-fuzz, customapp-import~110< 2 sNone. Plain objects in, plan out; injected now
Integration (in-process API)customapp-api, customapp-scope, customapp-public, customapp-outbox, customapp-sync, customapp-offline, customapp-acceptance, customapp-reuse~85< 6 sReal openDb(tmp) + createApi; fakeSmtp; real rate limiter with limits ON
Socket race (real HTTP)customapp-race (spawns server.js, parallel fetch)4~3 sNone; real process
E2E (browser)journeys.mjs: "Event night, four devices"1 journey, ~10 checks~40 sNone; real browser contexts
Load (manual + rehearsal)scripts/event-load.mjs (npm run event:load)1 scenario2 to 5 minNone; prod-like copy, limits ON

Roughly 55 % unit, 40 % integration, 5 % race and browser. The integration share is higher than the textbook pyramid on purpose. In CORE, an in-process API test is as fast as a unit test (no network, a tmp file), and it exercises permissions, module gating and audit, which a pure test cannot.

Appendix C: Invariants (shared checkInvariants, draft for Prover)

  1. For every active table: confirmed + activeReservations + activeHolds <= capacity (10 for YEP).
  2. No two active seat claims share (tableId, seatNo) when a seat number is set.
  3. A guest holds at most one active seat claim (by construction if the seat is embedded in the guest).
  4. A substitute and its original never both hold an active seat claim.
  5. Every guest and every table has 0 or 1 owner. The owner is an active event-staff member of that event.
  6. Exactly one superAdminUserId per event, and it is an active user.
  7. checkedInAt is set at most once per guest. Corrections are recorded as events, not overwrites.
  8. A seat claim's table is eligible for the guest's classification (preferred / regular / employee

/ mixed rules).

  1. A deactivated or removed table has no active claims.
  2. A preferred reservation is released only by a release event with an actor (no expiry path exists).
  3. seq is strictly increasing, and every state change has exactly one change-log entry.
  4. Status axes are independent: RSVP, check-in, assignment and notification. Email failure never

changes assignment.

Appendix D: Test specifications (testing-strategy Step 4)

Unit
File: test/customapp-engine.test.js            Layer: Unit (pure plan())
  assign: confirms into a table with 9 claims; refuses FULL at 10 counting reservations + holds
  assign: refuses SEAT_TAKEN for an occupied seat number; refuses NOT_ELIGIBLE (employee table, regular guest)
  assign: refuses STALE when expect.guestRev != current rev; refusal carries fresh options
  assign: refuses OUT_OF_SCOPE when actor owns the guest but not the table, and vice versa
  check-in: sets checkedInAt from injected now; second check-in refuses ALREADY_CHECKED_IN
  preferred check-in: returns the reserved seat; released reservation routes to reassignment queue
  transfer: old seat kept until new seat validated (FULL target leaves guest where they were)
  swap: two guests at two full tables succeed atomically; swap across scopes refuses for staff, succeeds for SA
  release: SA releases a preferred no-show; staff refuses unless delegated release is enabled for the event
  group assign: fits 4 into a table with 4 free; 5 into the same refuses with alternatives; split requires confirm flag
  substitute: links to original, moves original's claim, original ends with none
  reallocate: pending guests move to new owner; confirmed claims untouched; old owner refused next write
  table admin: renumber/reclassify ok; deactivate with claims refuses TABLE_HAS_ASSIGNMENTS
  every case: afterEach assert.deepEqual(checkInvariants(state), [])
Mocks: none. Edge: capacity 1 table, 0 tables, guest with no industry, unknown command type.

  same industry ranks above related above other; full and ineligible tables never listed
  ties broken by remaining capacity then table number (deterministic order, so tests are stable)
  exclusion tag removes a table; group-fit ranks only tables with enough room for all members
  scope filter: staff see only delegated tables in "recommended"; read-only "others" list separate

Appendix F: Coverage targets (testing-strategy Step 5)

No coverage tool in CORE (zero dependencies). node --test --experimental-test-coverage exists in Node 22 and needs no install. Use it locally as a report, not as a gate.

CategoryTargetRationale
engine.js, invariants.js, rank.js95 %+ lines, every refusal branchThe event's correctness lives here; pure, so cheap to cover
commit.js, routes.js, public.js85 %+Permission, nonce, gating and public-surface contracts
outbox.js, sync75 %+Failure paths matter more than the happy path
Views (public/js/views/customapp.js, guest page)Journey + phone sweepNo DOM unit tests in CORE; follow the existing pattern

Appendix G: Quality gates

  • Per edit (developer loop): node --test test/customapp-*.test.js in under 5 s, which includes

fuzz at 50 seeds. CUSTOMAPP_FUZZ_SEEDS=1000 runs locally before a release.

  • Per push (existing CI, unchanged workflow): the full npm test (the event files add about

10 s) plus npm run journeys (adds the one journey) must pass before the deploy job. Nothing new to configure.

  • Release (per tier, Strategist's dates): the tier's acceptance rows are green; the version is

bumped; the devlog entry, docs/guides/events.md and the whats-new.md line ship in the same commit; screenshots retaken with npm run guides:screenshots -- <file>.

  • Freeze candidate (6 Dec): full fuzz at 1000 seeds, race test ×20 runs (flake check),

event:load on a prod copy with limits ON, and a tagged commit. After the freeze, fixes only, each with a test that failed before it.

  • Event morning (read-only): GET /api/customapp/events/:id/health returns

checkInvariants() == [] on production, and the SA dashboard shows a banner if it ever turns non-empty during the night.

  • Review checklist for every event PR:
  • no await in engine.js or commit.js;
  • every write uses mutation();
  • every refusal uses the catalogue;
  • new rules are added to checkInvariants();
  • tests assert codes, not message text;
  • no YEP-specific string in server/customapp.

Appendix H: n8n patterns from a quality lens

  • Waiting-form status page (Architect and Scout verified: waiting-forms.ts, timingSafeEqual

on the resume token). It gives a clean, testable contract: opaque token, uniform 401/404, a status suffix to poll. Copy the contract into customapp-public.test.js rows.

  • Typed push messages (Architect verified: abstract.push.ts). A small discriminated set of

message types ({type:'changed', seq}) keeps the browser handler a single switch. Share the type names from shared/customapp/ so server and client cannot drift.

  • Concurrency control as a separate, testable service (unverified detail): n8n isolates its

execution concurrency limit in its own service class with its own unit tests, rather than burying it in handlers. Do the same with the outbox throttle: outbox.js alone, fakeSmtp in tests.

  • Do not borrow: queue mode and multi-main locking. They would invalidate the single-thread

atomicity that the whole test strategy relies on.

Tension pairs

OperatorVSStrategist: size of the event-day operations layer vs 12-day feature scopeAdvocateVSSkeptic: frictionless guest verification vs spoofing and enumerationArchitectVSOperator: event data in shared db.json vs a separate event store for recoveryScoutVSStrategist: reserved and held seat states vs no holds

Round 2: Challenge

Advocate responds to SkepticComplete
View challenge

Advocate Response to Skeptic

Their position: Surname + last 4 can be spoofed by a colleague who knows an office line or a business-card number. Skeptic wants one identical reply for every failure (no match, ambiguous, locked out), an activation window from doors-open, per-surname and per-event failure caps, a random status token stored hashed instead of status re-derived from the identifiers, and a repeat self-check-in that flags staff and "does not re-show the seat".

My response: Modify.

Reasoning: Skeptic and I disagree less than the framing suggests. Skeptic's own Appendix B lists the surname normalisation I asked for (NFKD, diacritics, spaces, hyphens, dots) as P0. My referral-code reply is already a uniform failure reply: "Please see the registration desk and show this code: R-482" looks the same whether the cause is no match, two matches, a lockout or bad input. Skeptic also lists remote spoofing as an accepted residual risk, caught at the door. So I accept most of Skeptic's controls, but each one has to fail toward the desk, never toward a dead end. Mrs. Dela Cruz at 6:40 PM must never see a lock screen. If she trips a control, the worst that happens is a visible code and a 20-second desk visit. I concede four things. (1) Every public failure gets the same HTTP status, the same copy, the same layout and similar timing, and differs only in the random referral code. The staff side shows the real reason ("5 wrong last-4 attempts on DELACRUZ"). (2) Normalisation is deterministic canonicalisation only. No fuzzy or edit-distance matching, no first-name fallback and no partial surnames on the public page. Those belong to staff-assisted search behind sign-in. Normalisation folds spelling variants of one surname. It does not merge different people, so it adds no meaningful multi-match. Multi-matches come from shared corporate lines, and they already go to the desk. (3) A 32-byte random status token, stored as SHA-256, is the primary way back to the status page. Re-verifying is only a fallback for a device without the token. (4) The activation window. Before doors open the page says "Registration opens at 6:00 PM". That is event-level information, so it leaks nothing about any guest. I hold firm where a control would strand real guests at peak. No per-event global lockout. One script or one crowd of fat-fingered guests could burn a global cap and stop everyone at 6:45 PM. A high global failure rate should alarm the Super Administrator, never block. The per-surname cap must forgive typos. Count distinct wrong last-4 values, so retrying "4417" three times counts once. Set it at about 5 distinct values per 10 minutes, then route that surname to the desk with the same uniform reply. Any staffer can clear the lock from the referral code in one tap. On a repeat verification, Skeptic's "do not re-show the seat" protects nothing. A spoofer sees the table on the first check-in anyway. Hiding it only punishes the real guest who switched phones or lost the tab. My compromise: re-verifying from a device without that guest's token shows the same status, and the record gets a "verified again from another device, 6:52 PM" flag in the owner's queue and the Super Administrator's alerts. Detection without scolding. For remote pre-arrival spoofing I would not use the typed rotating desk code: all 300 guests would pay a per-guest typing cost to stop a rare attack. I prefer a zero-typing presence key in the QR URL itself, printed fresh for event day (/event/yep26?k=…) and rotatable by the Super Administrator if a photo leaks. I defer to Skeptic and Operator on whether that is P0 or P1.

Merged guest-verification spec (proposed for convergence)

Non-negotiable for me: no control produces a guest-facing lock screen, "not found", "already checked in" or a different message per failure. Every control ends at the desk with a code that pre-fills the staffer's search.

Needed from others: Craftsman, a journey test for each row above, including "locked surname cleared at desk in one action". Tuner, timing parity between the match and failure paths under the single-IP load test.

Architect responds to OperatorComplete
View challenge

Architect Response to Operator

Their position: Putting the event inside CORE's shared db.json turns event recovery into whole-company recovery. The only restore path rolls all of CORE back to an hourly snapshot. That loses up to an hour of check-ins and also rolls back HR, CRM and projects. Meanwhile, on a 1 GB t3.micro, every check-in re-stringifies the whole of CORE while about 300 phones poll. The event therefore needs event-scoped snapshots and a replayable log, so that one event can be restored onto a standby. Skeptic lists a separate data file as an open decision.

My response: Modify

Reasoning: I concede the storage location. I hold the write discipline.

Operator is right, and re-reading the code gives one more reason that I missed in Round 1. Admin panel → "Restore a backup" (server/api.js:1314, db.replace at :422) replaces all of db.data. An administrator restoring CORE for an unrelated reason on 9 December would silently roll back or erase every check-in. Three other things also argue for moving out:

  • save() cost grows with CORE, not with the event.
  • Guest PII would sit in 30 nightly whole-CORE copies.
  • The purge after the event could not be done cleanly.

So the event moves into its own store, the same way audit-log.jsonl and documents/ already live beside db.json. My Round 1 rule, "only above about 20 MB", was the wrong trigger. The bounded context and its recovery unit should decide where the data lives, not file size.

What I do not give up:

  1. One authoritative copy. No dual-writing to db.json and the event file.
  2. One synchronous commit() that re-reads the role, checks revisions and invariants, applies the change and persists it in one event-loop turn. Nothing is awaited between the check and the write.
  3. No cross-store transactions. A commit() writes only its event's store, plus the audit append it already makes. Anything that touches db.json, such as creating an Event-staff account, is a separate CORE operation. Event code refers to users by id and re-reads them at every commit.

I would not build a log that is just a rewritten array inside a JSON file. I would build a small write-ahead pair per event: a compact state.json rewritten on every commit (about 300-500 KB, a few ms) and an append-only log.jsonl. That makes the per-commit cost independent of both CORE's size and the event's history. The same log is Operator's replay journal and my live-sync cursor.

The cost I accepted in Round 1 now has to be paid, and it is bounded:

  • Add the event directory to the offsite manifest allow-list (server/offsite.js:176,265), following the documents/ precedent at :280-290.
  • Add one cron line.
  • Make the restore check parse the event store.
  • Add a RESTORE.md section.

All of this can ship by 1 December, which leaves the 6 December chaos drill to prove it.

Revised storage contract (replaces Appendix B "collections in db.json")
data/customapp/<eventId>/
  state.json     compact JSON {v, eventId, seq, event, tables[], guests[], outbox[]}; tmp + fsync + rename on every commit
  log.jsonl      one line per commit: {seq, at, actor, op, args, after:{guests:[...], tables:[...]}, prevHash}
  snapshots/     state-<HHMM>.json every 5 min while event.window is open (copy of the last serialized bytes; no re-stringify)
db.json          keeps only: modules.customapp switch, customApp.events index [{id, code, name, dir, status}]
ConcernRule
Commit orderValidate, then apply in memory, then append log.jsonl (fsync), then write state.json (tmp+rename). If the state write fails: undo in memory, append {seq, void:true}, return 503. If even that append fails, set the event to readOnly and alert.
BootLoad state.json, then apply after images from log lines with seq > state.seq that are not voided. This covers a crash between the two writes.
Replay semanticsPoint-in-time restore applies after-images up to seq N, which is deterministic and does not re-validate. Paper reconciliation replays commands through commit(), which re-validates so capacity and duplicates are still checked. Same log, two uses.
IntegrityprevHash chains the log lines, using the server/audit.js pattern. The restore check verifies the chain and that state.seq is no greater than the last log seq.
Event restorePOST /api/customapp/events/:id/restore (event SA + CORE admin, password re-entry, requires readOnly). It loads a snapshot or export, then replays the log to a chosen seq. It touches only this event's directory. It never calls db.replace.
StandbyCopy the event export (state.json plus log.jsonl), import it on standby, and the seq continues from there. Same format as the 5-minute laptop download.
Full-CORE restoredb.replace leaves data/customapp/ alone. The Admin restore screen says "Event data is not part of this backup" whenever an event is open. A test asserts that a CORE restore does not change any event's seq.
BackupsHourly offsite: customapp/<eventId>/{state.json,log.jsonl} go into the manifest with sha256. Nightly cron: rsync of data/customapp/ to backups/customapp/, 30 days or the event retention date, whichever comes first.
Purge (Guardian)Deleting the event removes its directory and its local snapshot and backup copies. Offsite copies age out under the bucket lifecycle. Retention is now per event, not tied to CORE's.
Live syncLong-poll on seq, unchanged. The cursor is now the log seq.

What this does not change: the data model inside the event (guest-embedded seat, tables, revisions, invariants I1 to I7), the routes, the n8n-derived outbox, and the single-process rule. Queue mode and clustering stay rejected, because single-thread atomicity still depends on one writer.

Still measure on 28 Nov: production db.json size and save() p95, because CORE-side writes (sign-ins, audit, notifications) still run on the same loop during the event. Event state.json write p95 must stay under 20 ms on the resized instance with 300 guests. The 4-5 Dec load test enforces that.

Operator responds to ArchitectComplete
View challenge

Operator Response to Architect

Their position: Keep the event in CORE's shared db.json (caEvents, caTables, caGuests, caLog). Every commit() ends in the synchronous whole-file db.save() so validation, change and write happen in one turn of the event loop. A separate data file is considered only if db.json grows past about 20 MB, because a second file needs backup, offsite-manifest and restore changes.

My response: Modify

Reasoning: Architect is right about where the data lives, and I withdraw any suggestion of a second store for this event. I checked the cost of a split. server/offsite.js:265 refuses any file other than db.json, audit-head.json and audit-log.jsonl. The nightly cron copies only db.json. RESTORE.md and the nightly restore check know of one data file. A split would therefore change the save path, the cron, the offsite seal, the restore check and the runbook, all in the 12 days before the event, and would give us a recovery path that has never been drilled. That is a worse 3 AM risk than the one it removes. The event also references db.data.users for staff roles, so one file keeps a single source of truth with no cross-file consistency to manage.

My objection was never about the file. It is about how finely we can restore. So I accept the shared db.json on four conditions, and each one is cheap:

  1. Restore by event, not by file. An event export/import replaces one event's ca* records inside the live db.json through one commit()-style write and leaves HR, CRM and projects alone. event.readOnly=true stays on while it runs. Restoring the whole db.json during the event is forbidden in the runbook unless the instance itself is lost.
  2. Event snapshots are derived copies, not a second store. Every 5 minutes during the event window, an event-only JSON file goes to data/backups/events/<event>-<HHMM>.json with its seq. The command-desk laptop also pulls it. These files are copies taken from db.json, so they need no change to the offsite manifest or the save path. That takes the recovery point from 60 minutes to 5, and to seconds once the caLog entries after the snapshot's seq are replayed. The same import, given that snapshot, is how we load the warm standby.
  3. The trigger is latency, and it has a date. Twenty MB is the wrong trigger. What matters is save() time on a 1 GB t3.micro while about 300 phones poll. On 28 Nov we measure production's db.json size and the save() p50 and p95. If the p95 on staging, with production-sized data, is above about 50 ms, we pull the cheap levers first, all before 3 Dec: write compact JSON instead of JSON.stringify(data, null, 2) (roughly halves the time and the bytes, and every reader is unaffected), coalesce outbox status writes, and resize to t3.small or t3.medium on 7 Dec. We split only if p95 stays above about 150 ms after that, and that call is made by 30 Nov so the backup and restore changes can be drilled on 6 Dec.
  4. Watch the shared blast radius. Because a bad write now endangers all of CORE, commit() must undo in memory when save() throws, as Architect's sketch already does. The event dashboard alerts when save() p95 goes above 250 ms or disk free falls below 1 GB. The chaos drill on 6 Dec proves an event restore onto standby in under 5 minutes.

The event snapshots hold guest PII, so they join the retention timer that starts on 10 Dec.

Scout responds to StrategistComplete
View challenge

Scout Response to Strategist

Their position: Short-lived seat holds are "Won't" (backlog #35) because one synchronous commit() leaves no time between the check and db.save(). Capacity still counts confirmed + reserved (backlog #7), and a preferred guest's pre-assigned seat stays blocked until someone releases it by hand (#6).

My response: Modify. I concede the held state. I keep reserved as a required, durable seat state, and the invariant and fuzz suite must cover it.

Reasoning: My Round 1 merged two things the spec keeps apart, so I correct it here. Section 8 makes reservations mandatory: "Count all confirmed assignments and active reservations toward the 10-person limit". Section 21 and the "Preferred guest / no-show" acceptance scenario say what a reservation is: a preferred guest's pre-assigned table and seat that "stays blocked until manual release". Holds are optional in the spec: "Temporary holds, if implemented" (s.8) and "Optional short-lived seat holds may reduce conflicts" (s.16). The pretix precedent I cited supports Strategist's cut. pretix counts unexpired cart positions toward a quota only because anonymous buyers spend minutes across many requests between choosing a seat and paying (pretix quotas). Seats.io's holdToken exists for the same reason: consumer checkout. Here a staffer chooses a table and confirms it in a single request, and the group-arrival case also assigns all or none in a single commit(). That leaves no checkout window to protect. A staffer's "pending pick" while talking to a guest can lose the race safely through pretix's other pattern, which I keep: a refusal with a reason (409 {reason:"table_full", revision}) and a refreshed list of eligible tables, as section 16 asks. A TTL hold would also add the only write that no request triggers (an expiry sweeper). That write would need its own audit record and restart recovery on the JSON DB, and it would bring back the time window that the synchronous commit removes. So the state machine is seat.status ∈ {reserved, confirmed} plus a released record in history, with no held.

Craftsman's worry that swap or transfer handlers will forget to count reserved seats is real, but the fix is structural, not a hold state. All capacity checks should go through one function, claimsOn(event, tableId), that counts guests whose seat.status is reserved or confirmed. The fuzz suite should generate preferred-guest reservations in its random operations: assign, transfer, swap, release, unlisted approval, substitution, group arrival. After every step it checks Craftsman's invariant 1 (with activeHolds = 0) and invariant 10 (a reservation is released only by an actor, never by expiry). One more test makes adding holds later safe: an exhaustiveness test that fails when SEAT_STATUSES holds a value that claimsOn does not classify as counting or not counting. If the client asks for holds after 5 Dec, the test forces whoever adds held to decide how it counts.

Net effect on the design:

  • Keep reserved (Must): it is part of backlog #6 and #7, it is created at import or by an administrator before the event, and it leaves only by release (with actor and reason) or by becoming confirmed at check-in.
  • Drop held / TTL holds (Won't, as Strategist proposed). Last-seat conflicts are handled by a refusal with a reason plus a refresh.
  • Invariant and fuzz: count reserved, assert that holds are 0, assert that no reservation is ever released by expiry, and add the status-exhaustiveness test.
Skeptic responds to AdvocateComplete
View challenge

Skeptic Response to Advocate

Their position: The guest flow should have as little friction as possible. That means surname plus last 4 of mobile, or the full email, with lenient Filipino-name normalisation. Re-verifying from any device should return the same status and table "with no scolding". Every failure should give a referral code (R-482) that pre-fills the desk search, never "not found".

My response: Modify

Reasoning: Most of what Advocate wants does not weaken the control. My Round 1 tied two things together that should be separate. Surname strictness is not a security control: the attacker in my threat model already knows the surname. The secret is the last 4 digits, and the control that matters is time (spec line 267: "registration activation"). So I accept lenient normalisation in full: NFKD, strip diacritics, lowercase, drop spaces, hyphens, periods and apostrophes, strip "Jr./Sr./III", and match stored aliases. This was already my own P0 edge case. I attach one condition: matching stays exact on the normalised form. No edit-distance or "did you mean" matching, because fuzzy matching is what would really increase multi-match and enumeration.

Advocate's claim that leniency creates many multi-match cases is weak. A collision needs both the same normalised surname and the same last 4. Among about 300 guests, that happens almost only on a shared corporate line. So we find these cases before the event: the import preflight lists every ambiguous pair and every guest with no mobile and no email. The Super Administrator then fixes the record (adds the full email, a direct mobile, or marks the guest "desk only"), and nothing is left to chance on the night.

I also accept the referral code. It fits with my uniform-reply rule as long as these conditions hold:

  • Every guest-level failure (no match, ambiguous, capped, unlisted) returns byte-identical copy and status code, and a code drawn from one random pool. There is no per-reason prefix and no difference in timing.
  • The server stores the reason next to the code, and only authenticated staff can see it.
  • The code carries only what the guest typed, never master-list data.

I withdraw one part of my Round 1: "a second self-check-in does not re-show the seat". Look at who could be harmed. A spoofer who already knows the surname and last 4 learns only a table number. The real guest who re-verifies on a second phone is the common case and should simply see their status. So re-verify is idempotent, has no scolding copy, and shows title or first name, table and seat only. It creates no second check-in, and it flags the record "verified again from another device" for staff. Advocate proposed that flag, and it replaces my scolding branch.

I maintain the hashed random status token, and I don't see it as opposed to re-verification. Each successful verify mints a new per-device token: 32 bytes, stored as SHA-256, revocable by staff. The status page polls with that token. The alternative, re-deriving status on every poll, would mean resending surname and last 4 every few seconds. That puts credentials in a polling loop and logs, and every typo-free poll would count against the failure caps.

On lockouts, I agree with Advocate's concern and modify my position. A hard per-surname lockout lets one person block "Dela Cruz" at the door. It becomes a soft cap:

  • 8 failed attempts per (event, normalised surname) in 15 minutes switch that surname to the uniform desk reply. The cap clears automatically after 15 minutes, and any staffer can clear it from the referral record.
  • The cost in the worst case is one guest walking about 10 metres to a desk that exists anyway.
  • An event-wide failure-rate alarm tells the Super Administrator about probing.

One more option is a rotating 4-digit desk code shown on the reception screen. It would be required only for a surname that is capped, or for any self-verify before doors-open. The real guest in the room passes straight through, and a remote spoofer cannot. This is P1 and only goes in if Strategist confirms there is time.

These conditions are non-negotiable for my Modify:

  • The activation window is enforced server-side, from doors-open. Before that, an event-level "opens at 6:00 PM" page is shown, and it accepts no input.
  • The preflight collision report is run and cleared before activation.
  • Guest-level failures have identical replies.
  • The status token is per device, and polling never sends surname or last 4.
  • The public limiter is keyed per event and per surname, not per IP, so the venue NAT does not turn my own control into the outage Advocate and I both found.

The residual risk is a pre-arrival spoof by someone who knows a colleague's direct mobile. This stays accepted. It is bounded by the activation window, the usher's "show this screen" step and the re-verify flag.
